Asia Maxima - Reverberating tremors
Investors increasingly focused last quarter on a dramatic sell-off in G7 government bond
markets, a renewed Federal Reserve tightening cycle and a renewed break out of hostilities
in the Middle East. But for now at least equities remain supported by stellar earnings growth
courtesy primarily of the still accelerating AI capex cycle.
The sell-off in long term government bonds reflects the surge in nominal GDP growth, most
particularly in America and Japan, as well as rising fiscal concerns. This has confirmed again
that G7 government bonds are in a structural bear market while Treasury Secretary Scott
Bessent’s failed efforts to suppress yields last quarter has renewed expectations of Japanese-
like policies of financial repression in the US, be it so-called Treasury QE or yield curve control.
The ten-year Treasury bond yield remains the most important price in world financial markets.
But the arrival of a new Fed chair has increased the importance of the American central
bank given Kevin Warsh’s stated commitment at the September FOMC meeting to hit the 2%
inflation target when inflation has now been above that target in the US for more than five years.
The key issue for US equities remains whether the hyperscalers will be able to make a return
on their massive AI-related investments. As these investments are increasingly funded by debt
rather than cash, the cycle is most likely to end when markets are no longer willing to fund
these investments. This risk grows the more interest rates rise. For this reason credit spreads
should be monitored closely. The long-term base case, or end game, remains massive capital
destruction in the US.
As regards geopolitics financial markets have, for the most part, remained remarkably calm
amidst continuing dramatic newsflow, be it concerning the Middle East or the Russian-Ukraine
conflict which intensified last quarter. Most importantly, the conflict with Iran remains far from
resolved.
China continues to post a record trade surplus reflecting its dominance in global
manufacturing and a related ongoing boom in intra-emerging market trade. Still there remains
a lack of consumption boosting measures even though Beijing is formally committed to
boosting the role of consumption. Indeed household borrowing is now declining in outright
terms.
Gold has continued to consolidate after last year’s historic rally. Still the world has moved to
a de facto gold standard as central banks continue to own more gold than US treasury bonds.
The base case is that the dollar debasement trade is only in temporary abeyance though the
actions of the Warsh Fed should be monitored closely.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
Global and regional overview 
If the AI capex trade remained in full throttle last quarter in terms of the accelerating spending, 
investors increasingly focused on a sell-off in G7 government bond markets, a renewed Federal 
Reserve tightening cycle and a renewed outbreak of hostilities in the Middle East. These all 
posed a growing challenge to equities even as Wall Street-correlated world stock markets 
remained well supported by stellar earnings growth, most particularly in the US. 
“The Hawk at Jackson Hole” was the headline of the Wall Street Journal editorial on Kevin 
Warsh’s speech in late August. The line of argument was that the speech was an effective 
response to those in the markets who had been doubting the inflation-fighting credibility of 
what the newspaper described as “America’s most hawkish central banker since Paul Volcker”. 
That may turn out to be the case. But such a judgement remains far from proven. Indeed a more 
prosaic interpretation of the speech was that it was an attempt to correct the impression 
created by the doveish message conveyed by Warsh in his post-FOMC press conference in late 
July. The change in language then led to the first Fed rate hike in September since July 2023 
with the federal funds rate increased by 25bp to 3.75-4.0%. Money markets ended last quarter 
expecting another 25bp of tightening by the end of this year. 
The case for tightening is clear. Both CPI and core PCE inflation, which in recent years has been 
the Fed’s favoured inflation measure, have been running above the Fed’s formal 2% target for 
the past 66 months (see following chart). For this reason inflation looks increasingly to be 
settling in the 3-4% range, which also is consistent with the acceleration in trend nominal GDP 
growth which also suggests higher long-term bond yields. US nominal GDP has risen at a trend 
growth rate of 5.8% YoY in the past 12 quarters and was up 6.3% YoY in 2Q26, compared with 
a trend rate of 4.7% YoY in the three years to 2019 (see following chart). 
US headline CPI inflation and core PCE inflation 
  
Source: Bureau of Labor Statistics, Bureau of Economic Analysis

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
US nominal GDP growth and 10-year Treasury bond yield 
  
Source: Bureau of Economic Analysis, Bloomberg 
 
Such a rise in long-term bond yields has now transpired with the US Treasury bond breaking 
above the psychologically important 5% level in September and ending last quarter at 5.28%. 
This sell-off poses a growing risk to equities, as discussed in the previous edition of this 
quarterly publication (see Asia Maxima 3Q26 – AI crescendo, 6 July 2026). It also occurred 
despite Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent’s failed efforts to draw a line in the sand last quarter 
by seeking to hold the 10-year yield at the 4.75% level. Amidst the Treasury’s continuing reliance 
on short term funding, Bessent’s message conveyed to the markets was that it was possible to 
increase buybacks at the long end dramatically. This jawboning was combined with leaked 
hints of even being prepared to deploy funds from the Treasury’s piggy bank, officially known 
as the Treasury General Account (TGA), to buy long-term Treasury bonds (see CNBC article: 
“Bessent could tap near $1 trillion Treasury General Account to fund bond buybacks, sources said”, 
24 August 2026).  
This triggered talk of Treasury QE as opposed to the former Fed-driven QE. While the Treasury’s 
announced increased buybacks, and stated willingness to buy more long term bonds if yields 
move higher, are not technically QE there are strong similarities. Meanwhile tapping the TGA to 
fund buybacks would be intrinsically inflationary, and also liquidity positive, as bank reserves 
would increase by the same amount as the TGA is reduced.   
Meanwhile the clear desire of the Treasury to control yields is fundamentally inflationary, in the 
dollar debasement sense. In this respect, Bessent is moving for now at least in the precise 
opposite direction of Warsh who stated plainly at the post-FOMC press conference in 
September after the rate hike that the Fed’s goal was to bring inflation down to the 2% level. 
More precisely, he said: “Today’s policy action will support a timelier return to the Committee’s 
2% goal . . . our predominant focus is on the price-stability side of our mandate. The plain fact 
is that inflation is too high and has been for too long”.  
Bessent’s focus on seeking to suppress yields also creates the impression among market 
participants that the key motivation is to control the federal government’s cost of debt servicing, 
just as has also been the case in Japan in terms of the Bank of Japan’s all too evident 
reluctance to raise rates more aggressively amidst surging nominal GDP growth. Japanese 
nominal GDP growth has been running at a trend rate of 4.2% YoY over the past 12 quarters, up 
from 0.9% in the 12 quarters to 4Q19 (see following chart).

Meanwhile, the minutes of the July FOMC meeting released on 19 August showed that there 
was substantial support within the committee for higher rates. The growing evidence since then 
that the Treasury Secretary wants to reduce long-term Treasury bond yields below market-
determined rates should, in theory at least, make the hawks at the Fed want to tighten monetary 
policy all the more. The other related point is that, if the long-term impact of AI should be 
disinflationary, the reality for now is that the still accelerating AI capex cycle in the US is 
inflationary, as reflected in the rising debt issuance of the hyperscalers and the related rise in 
credit spreads. The five hyperscalers, including Oracle, have issued US$229bn worth of bonds 
so far this year, up from US$108bn in 2025 (see following chart). Amazon, Alphabet and Meta’s 
corporate bond spreads over Treasuries have risen from 60bp, 51bp and 80bp in early June to 
75bp, 67bp and 93bp respectively (see following chart). So, the hyperscalers’ demand for long-
term funding is now competing with the federal government.

Meanwhile, from a philosophical standpoint, the most interesting point about Warsh’s Jackson 
Hole speech in late August was his comment that “money has something important to do with 
monetary policy”. This is a welcome development given the extent to which modern day central 
banks have been happy to ignore money supply-related data in recent years. This has been 
extraordinary even allowing for the fact that, as the Wall Street Journal editorial noted, financial 
technology has changed the definition of money supply and impacted the related issue of 
velocity. 
What is crystal clear is that if anyone at the Fed had looked at the surge in the money supply 
related charts in 2020-2021 as a consequence of the Covid-triggered monetisation (see 
following chart), they would have had a much better idea that inflationary risks were building. 
Remember that the Powell Fed did not start raising rates until March 2022.  
US M2 growth  
  
Source: Federal Reserve, Jefferies  
 
Returning to the state of the American economy the reliance on the key current drivers of 
growth, namely AI capex and wealth generation from the stock market, is evident from the latest 
data on compensation of employees from the national accounts. This rose by 1.1% YoY in real 
terms in August, down from 3.6% YoY in December 2023. Compensation of employees is 
defined as wages and salaries and related supplements such as employer contributions for 
employee pension and insurance funds and government social insurance. By contrast, real 
personal consumption rose by 2.6% YoY in August compared with 3.3% YoY in December 2023 
(see following chart). As a result, the personal savings rate has declined from 7.2% of

This weakening trend in ordinary salaried compensation is another symptom of America’s K-
shaped economy. Clearly, the economy will be vulnerable if the AI capex boom suddenly 
implodes given that AI capex has accounted for 1.10ppts or 50% of US real GDP growth of 2.2% 
YoY in 2Q26 (see following chart). For now there is no sign of that with the four hyperscalers’ 
capital spending estimated by consensus to rise from US$723bn this year to US$993bn next 
year (see following chart). But even if the tech evangelists’ most bullish forecasts on the 
monetisation of AI are fulfilled, wage compensation will still be under pressure precisely 
because of that success which will mean more can be produced with less labour input in a new 
era of “plenty”.  
On that point, labour income as a share of US output reached a record low of 52.8% in 2Q26 
according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics and, based on the predictions of the likes of 
Anthropic co-founder and CEO Dario Amodei, it will soon be heading much lower (see following 
chart). At some point that has to have extremely negative deflationary implications for 
consumer lending portfolios servicing the salaried middle-class. But for now at least the impact 
of the AI capex cycle is inflationary because of the physical inputs required, most importantly 
energy in the world outside China.

8

 
      
Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
Meanwhile, yield curve control (YCC) has always been the likely end game in America in terms 
of the direction of travel towards the demise of the US dollar paper standard, a point made on 
several occasions in the now 30-year history of this quarterly publication. Still, it was always 
likely that the authorities would try more subtle forms of yield suppression before resorting to 
a crude fixing of yields Japanese style. The 10-year JGB yield was fixed at “around 0%” from 
September 2016 to early 2022.  
Japan 10-year JGB yield and Yen/US$ (inverted scale) 
  
Source: Bloomberg 
 
As the Japanese example has made clear, any successful effort to suppress yields takes the 
selling pressure off the bond market and onto the currency. As for equities, liquidity boosting 
suppression of yields is bullish for equities but at the expense of the relevant currency. This is 
best shown by the contrasting performance of the Topix in yen terms and gold terms since the 
initial launch of YCC in Japan in September 2016. Since then, the Topix has risen by 212% in 
yen terms but is down 36% in gold terms (see following chart). 
Topix in yen terms and gold terms 
  
Source: Bloomberg, Jefferies 
 
Meanwhile so long as yields are not fixed Treasury bonds, along with other G7 government 
bonds, remain in a structural bear market which commenced in March 2020 after a 39-year bull 
market (see following chart).

US 10-year Treasury bond yield (monthly log chart)

One reason for this longstanding view is the return of inflation to the system, the consequence 
primarily of the Covid induced monetisation, and aggravated by other secular factors such as 
the retreat from globalisation and the laws of comparative advantage which prevailed in the so-
called neo-liberal era. The other is continuing fiscal deterioration in the G7 world, most 
importantly in the US.  
US public debt outstanding 
  
Source: US Treasury 
 
On that point, the US federal debt hit a key psychological landmark last quarter when the total 
debt reached US$40tn in mid-August. It is now US$40.17tn (see previous chart). A closer look 
at the data shows continuing deterioration as the positive impact of tariff revenue has fallen 
out of the data. The fiscal deficit for the first 11 months of last fiscal year (October 2025 – 
August 2026) was US$1.966tn, surpassing the full-year FY25 (ended 30 September 2025) 
deficit of US$1.775tn, though it is marginally below the deficit of US$1.973tn in the 11 months 
ended 31 August 2025 which was the highest 11-month deficit since October 2020-August 
2021, a time when the pandemic was raging (see following chart). The annualised fiscal deficit 
to GDP also rose to 6.1% in July though it declined to 5.6% of GDP in August (see following 
chart).

Global and regional overview 
US fiscal balance (11 months: October-August) 
  
Note: Fiscal years ending 30 September. Source: Bureau of the Fiscal Service, US Treasury, Bloomberg 
 
US annualised fiscal balance 
  
Source: Bureau of the Fiscal Service, Bloomberg, Jefferies 
 
Meanwhile, annualised net interest payments and entitlements as a percentage of government 
receipts rose to 98.4% in the 12 months to July, the highest level since the record 99.7% in the 
12 months to June 2020 when the Fed was engaged in Covid-triggered monetisation, and was 
95.7% in the 12 months to August (see following chart).  
The impact of tariffs, or the lack of tariff revenue, is easily shown. The monthly tariff number 
was a negative US$8.5bn in July following a negative US$25.56bn in June, compared with a 
positive US$22.12bn in April and a record US$31.35bn in October 2025, though it rose to a 
positive US$12.84bn in August (see following chart). As a result, total federal government 
receipts have declined by 1.6% YoY in the past three months to August. By contrast, total 
government outlays were up 5.0% YoY in the same three month period (see following chart), 
with national defence expenditure rising by 7.7% YoY in the three months to August. 
This fiscal deterioration is clearly one of the forces putting upward pressure on long-term 
Treasury bond yields, along with the acceleration in nominal GDP growth.

If the 10-year Treasury bond yield ended last quarter at the highest level since June 2007, the 
30-year yield at 5.63% was the highest level since June 2002 (see following chart). As for the 
two year Treasury yield, it was at 4.89%, or 101bp above the midpoint of the federal funds rate 
target range which implies more rate hikes are coming (see following chart). 
US 10-year and 30-year Treasury bond yields 
  
Source: Bloomberg 
 
US 2-year Treasury note yield and Federal funds rate 
  
Source: Bloomberg 
 
This sell-off at the long end was despite massive funding at the short end and continuing 
buybacks at the long end. Gross issuance of Treasury bills has accounted for 84.5% of gross 
marketable Treasury issuance in the 12 months to August (see following chart). While the US 
Treasury bought back US$92.1bn of Treasuries in 2Q26 and US$59.5bn last quarter (see 
following chart).  
Meanwhile, another issue is the growing pressure on foreign owners of Treasury bonds to sell, 
most particularly the biggest owners who are the Japanese. The Treasury’s latest data shows 
that Japan (including official and private investors) held US$1.10tn of US Treasuries at the end 
of July, with US$1.02tn in long-term Treasuries and US$80bn short-term. This is down from a 
recent high of US$1.24tn at the end of February (see following chart).

14

 
      
Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
That Bessent is well aware of this risk is clear to us from his decision to intervene in support of 
the yen in late July, even without demanding a BoJ rate hike as a quid pro quo. There have been 
two further signals which highlighted the Treasury Secretary’s rising angst on this issue. The 
first was his comment that it was “reasonable” for the Fed to consider increasing the size of its 
Foreign and International Monetary ⁠Authority (FIMA) repo facility with Japan (see Reuters 
article: “US Treasury's Bessent: Reasonable for Fed to consider upsizing FIMA”, 4 August 2026). 
The facility allows approved foreign central banks to raise dollars without selling their Treasury 
holdings in the open market. The current limit for the facility is US$60bn per counterparty. An 
interesting point here is that FIMA is a credit facility introduced during the pandemic as a 
liquidity backstop. It has nothing to do with forex intervention.  
Such a flexible approach contrasts with Kevin Warsh’s stated desire before becoming Fed 
chairman to shrink the Fed balance sheet. On that point the Fed balance sheet is, for now at 
least, expanding again, rising by US$207bn or 3.2% from the recent low of US$6.536tn in early 
December 2025 to US$6.743tn at the end of last quarter (see following chart). 
Federal Reserve balance sheet 
  
Note: Data up to the week ended 30 September 2026. Source: Federal Reserve 
 
The FIMA manoeuvre should be seen as a signal that Bessent is reluctant to see foreign central 
banks sell their foreign exchange reserves. A further signal is that the Treasury reportedly 
funded the intervention to buy yen with euros not dollars (see Financial Times article: “US euro 
sale to prop up yen blindsided ECB”, 7 August 2026). 
The above manoeuvring is necessary because of America’s acute dependence on foreign 
capital reflected in its extreme net international investment position (NIIP), a situation which 
many investors in America, particularly retail ones, appear to be completely unaware of. The US 
NIIP deficit has surged from US$7.8tn or 39.9% of GDP at the end of 2017 to a record US$22.4tn 
or 70.6% of GDP at the end of 2Q26, having been in deficit since 1989 (see following chart). 
This data point measures the difference between US residents’ foreign financial assets and 
foreigners’ holdings of US assets. 
Japan is in the opposite situation. Japan’s NIIP surplus has risen from Y329tn or 58.3% of GDP 
at the end of 2017 to a record Y562tn or 84.3% of GDP at the end of 2025 and was Y561tn or 
82.7% of GDP at the end of 2Q26 (see following chart). This is why the potential for a huge 
repatriation of capital into Japan is real.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
For now foreigners own US equities because of the hot AI story which, to be fair, has continued 
to deliver handsome returns, most particularly for those invested in the picks and shovels plays. 
However, the opposite is the case with US Treasury bonds which have been in a brutal bear 
market since March 2020 since when they have declined by 42%, or an annualised 7.9%, based 
on the Bloomberg US Long (10Y+) Treasury bond index. By contrast, the S&P500 has risen by 
276%, or an annualised 22.5% on a total-return basis, since its March 2020 low (see following 
chart). 
Bloomberg US Long (10Y+) Treasury bond index vs S&P500 total-return performance 
  
Source: Bloomberg 
 
A further aggravating factor for the financial markets, most particularly the bond markets, has 
been continuing geopolitical tensions. However much investors may want to ignore the 
newsflow from the Middle East, at certain points in time it becomes impossible to ignore.  
As regards Iran, Donald Trump’s frustration with the trap he is in has become ever more self-
evident during the past quarter as his polling has continued to deteriorate. A Reuters/Ipsos poll 
conducted between 17-20 September shows that 62% of Americans disapprove of US military 
strikes on Iran while 67% disapprove of Trump’s handling of the economy. As for Trump’s 
overall job approval rating, it declined to 38.6% at the end of last quarter, down from 52.3% in 
late January 2025, according to the RealClearPolitics poll average (see following chart). This 
means his and the Republicans’ prospects for the Mid-term elections on 3 November look 
ominous.

17

 
      
Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
The latest strategy of the Trump administration seems to be to hope that growing economic 
pressures will cause Tehran to return to the negotiating table. This writer would not bet on that. 
Tehran has every incentive to keep the pressure on until the mid-term elections which are now 
only five weeks away. The other important point, not well reported in the Western media, is that 
Iranian Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei on 9 August put his own trusted choice Mohsen 
Rezaei in the key executive role of Secretary of the Supreme National Security Council. The so-
called moderates, such as President Masoud Pezeshkian, have been sidelined for the simple 
reason that the MoU signed with the Trump administration was broken from almost the day it 
was signed by the US president on 17 June, specifically by Israel’s renewed attacks on Lebanon. 
The result is that the Strait of Hormuz remains essentially closed to most ships. Meanwhile the 
price gap between crude oil and refined products, such as diesel, has become ever wider. The 
so-called Diesel Crack Spread, which measures the price difference between diesel futures and 
WTI crude oil futures, rose to a record US$118/bbl in mid-September and was US$107/bbl at 
the end of last quarter (see following chart). 
Diesel Crack Spread  
  
Source: Bloomberg 
 
So, Trump remains in a trap of his own making. He has, essentially, two choices. One is to 
abandon the entire Iran war effort, leaving the countries in the region to sort out their problems 
themselves. After all the US is energy independent. However sensible this might be, and it would 
certainly be popular with the American electorate judging by the polling, this seems unlikely. 
The other is to escalate with renewed talk of boots on the ground and the like. Meanwhile, Iran 
has made clear that if attacks on Iran civilian infrastructure resume it will respond by attacking 
infrastructure in the Gulf states, including desalination plants and airports, and not just US 
bases. Meanwhile, the damage done to US military bases in the Gulf has by all accounts been 
extensive. At least 20 US military sites in the Middle East have reportedly been severely 
damaged since the start of the war.  
If this is the state of play in the Middle East, there is also the Russian-Ukraine conflict and the 
related collateral damage. Western media has focused in recent months on the damage done 
by drones to Russian oil refineries and the like. But much less discussed, if at all, has been the 
escalating Russian blockade last quarter of Odessa and other neighboring ports in the Black 
Sea. This is curbing Ukraine’s ability to export creating the risk that the country has become 
essentially land locked. The impact can be seen in rising grain prices. The Bloomberg 
Commodity Grains subindex has risen by 16% from the recent low in late June, though down 
9% from the recent high in early September (see following chart).

Indeed, the impact on prices of both ongoing conflicts should not be underestimated. There 
remains a big difference, for example, between the price of crude oil quoted in world financial 
markets and what people are actually paying in real life. This is because rising diesel prices 
affect the cost of freight, agriculture, construction and mining among other things. So, inflation 
is kicking in through higher freight, industrial fuel and transportation costs, which are not 
immediately reflected in the global price of a barrel of oil. Another related issue is the dramatic 
decline in LNG shipments from Qatar.  Qatar has exported as of August only 18 LNG cargoes 
outside the Gulf since the conflict began in March, down 96% YoY from 509 in the same period 
last year (see following chart). 
Meanwhile, the line from Tehran has been that it is only willing to resume negations with 
America on the basis of the MOU signed by Trump in June. At a minimum the Iranians will want 
to see some of their estimated over US$100bn of frozen assets returned before any deal is 
made.   
LNG cargoes delivered from Oatar 
  
Source: ICIS 
 
Staying on geopolitics, the view long expressed in this quarterly publication has been that 
German politics is the key fault line in Europe since Germany sits at the core of the Eurozone. 
For this reason, the result of the Saxony-Anhalt state election in early September is important 
even if the state, located in the western part of the former East Germany, only accounts for 2.5% 
of the German population.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
It is important not only because the Alternative für Deutschland (AfD) received 43.8% of the 
vote but also because of the sheer scale of the collapse of the CDU vote. The CDU got only 
17.2% compared with 37.1% in the 2021 state election. By contrast, the AfD vote surged from 
20.8% to 43.8% (see following chart). It is also important that the voter turnout rose from 60.3% 
in 2021 to 77.8%, the highest ever recorded in a Saxony-Anhalt state election since German 
reunification in 1990. The AfD won 39 of the 83 seats in the state, compared with only 15 for 
the CDU, though still just short of an outright majority. 
Result of Saxony-Anhalt state election on 6 September 2026 (% of vote) 
  
Source: State Portal of Saxony-Anhalt 
 
This result reflects the reality that growing numbers of votes are opting for the AfD, not because 
they are devotees of the “far right”, as the mainstream media keep portraying them, but rather 
because there is otherwise no party really representing the views of a conservative voter, be it 
on immigration, the excesses of Germany’s welfare state or Ukraine. On that last point the 
popularity rating of CDU leader and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz has imploded since he 
was elected 17 months ago in May 2025, plunging from 42% in July 2025 to a record low of 
10%, according to the latest poll conducted by Infratest Dimap between 28-30 September (see 
following chart). By comparison Donald Trump, with a 38% approval rating, looks relatively 
popular.  
Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s approval rating 
  
Note: *Latest poll on 28-30 September 2026. Source: Infratest dimap, ARD-Deutschland TREND Survey

is undertaking its largest rearmament programme since 1955. Defence spending is expected 
to reach €109bn next year and €184bn or 3.5% of GDP by 2030. So, Merz is most associated 
with the twin policies of pushing rearmament and funding Ukraine. The European Union has 
reportedly already lent €15.7bn to Ukraine so far this year, with no collateral attached, including

€11.7bn for defence procurement and €4bn for budget support. These disbursements are part

unpopular with Germans on both the right and the left, not least because of surging gas prices.

an 18% decline in US natural gas prices. As a result, the ratio of German gas prices to US gas

advocates turning back on Russian energy and ending the war in Ukraine.

This sort of data illustrates how the impact of the Iran and Ukraine conflicts are beginning to

standpoint the AfD is currently commanding a 28% support rating in the opinion polls compared 
with only 21% for the ruling CDU (see following chart). While the German establishment parties 
can continue to maintain the stance that they will have nothing to do with the AfD, the so-called 
“firewall policy”, this stance has become increasingly impractical to maintain for the simple 
reason that it seems guaranteed to annoy a growing number of German voters.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
Meanwhile, amidst the above negatives, be it falling bond prices, rising inflation pressures and 
shifting geopolitics, there is no doubt that the resilience of the US stock market is best explained 
by strong earnings momentum.  The LSEG I/B/E/S consensus data as of 25 September shows 
that S&P500 3Q26 earnings are expected to rise by 30.2% YoY. The IT sector has the second-
highest forecast 3Q26 earnings growth of 65.1% reflecting the strength of the AI capex cycle 
(see following chart). All 12 sub-industries in the IT sector have higher forecast earnings than 
a year ago, with the semiconductor and semiconductor materials & equipment sub-industries 
having the highest earning growth (131.7% and 68.9%, respectively). If these two sub-industries 
are excluded, the forecast growth rate for the IT sector declines to 25.3%.  
S&P500 3Q26 earnings growth forecast revision by sector 
  
Note: As of 25 September 2026. Source: LSEG I/B/E/S 
 
If there is a negative point to be aware of it is that the free cash flow generation of the S&P500 
in aggregate has begun to deteriorate, reflecting primarily the continuing capex binge by the 
four main hyperscalers, which still account for 18.3% of the index. The S&P500 free cash flow 
yield has declined from 4.76% in October 2022 to 2.91%, though up from a recent low of 2.51% 
in October 2025 (see following chart). Meanwhile, the four major hyperscalers’ aggregate free 
cash flow has declined from US$60bn in 4Q25 to only US$7bn in 2Q26 and is forecast by 
consensus to fall to a negative US$13bn in 3Q26, while their aggregate capex has risen from 
US$72bn in 1Q25 to US$165bn in 2Q26 and an estimated US$200bn in 3Q26 (see following 
chart). 
S&P500 free cash flow yield 
  
Source: Bloomberg

If this explains the slight underperformance year-to-date of the hyperscalers, and their recent 
derating in terms of valuations, the AI capex arms race continues. And that remains for now 
extremely earnings-accretive because, as highlighted in the previous edition of this quarterly 
publication, the picks and shovels plays such as semiconductor stocks book their profits 
upfront while the hyperscalers remain in no hurry to account fully for their capex, though their 
depreciation bill is rising. The four major hyperscalers’ depreciation expenses were US$43bn in 
2Q26, up 26% YoY (see following chart). Meanwhile, the four hyperscalers’ share prices have 
risen by 8.2% year-to-date, compared with an 11.8% gain in the S&P500 (see following chart). 
They are now trading at 21.3x 12-month forward earnings, down from 25.5x at the start of this 
year (see following chart).   
Then there is the key issue of the hyperscalers’ growing cloud revenues. The aggregate cloud 
revenues for Microsoft, Google, and Amazon rose by 38% YoY to US$126bn in 2Q26 (see 
following chart). This is the main reason why investors are still hoping that an adequate return 
will be made on the soaring AI capex as the hyperscalers turn into data factories.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
Still an interesting question is how much of these hyperscalers’ cloud revenues are accounted 
for by the cash-burning large language models which, as customers, would seem potentially 
much more risky than regular corporates. On this point, the hyperscalers have not disclosed 
that much specific data. But estimates from Jefferies’ US tech analysts, based on known data 
points, reveal the following. The top two frontier labs, OpenAI and Anthropic, accounted for 
roughly 6% of Amazon Web Services’ (AWS) total cloud revenues in 2Q26, a figure which 
includes its legacy non-AI cloud services. Jefferies’ US tech analysts expect that to increase 2-
3x to 12-18% in 3Q26, while the two frontier labs accounted for an estimated roughly 45% of 
Google’s Cloud Platform revenues in 2Q26 and 25% of Microsoft’s Azure. 
If this is the backdrop the key question remains if and when markets will really start to question 
the returns from AI. This is in the context of the longstanding base case of this quarterly 
publication, namely that the most likely longer term outcome of the AI story in market terms 
will be massive capital destruction in the US with market share going to cheaper open-source 
Chinese large language models. In the meantime, the view has been since the start of 2023, 
when the AI story began in stock market terms, that the best AI-related investment was the 
picks and shovels trade, in terms of the companies who are profiting directly from the 
hyperscalers’ spending. This remains the view.  
Meanwhile, the latest data from OpenRouter shows that Chinese models continue to grow in 
popularity. The top Chinese AI models processed 76.12tn tokens on the global aggregator 
platform OpenRouter in the week ended 27 September, up from 4.37tn in late April, based on 
the weekly usage of the top nine models on OpenRouter. This compared with 14.2tn tokens for 
the top US models (see following chart). The already growing focus on this issue only 
intensified last quarter with the launch of Moonshot AI’s open-source Kimi K3 on 17 July which, 
to quote AI jargon, is “competing at the frontier” with an estimated 95% of the performance of 
Anthropic’s Claude Fable 5. 
Weekly usage of top nine AI models across OpenRouter 
  
Note: Data up to the week ended 27 September 2026. Source: OpenRouter 
 
This is the latest manifestation of a story which began with the so-called DeepSeek moment in 
late January 2025, in terms of the growing popularity and competitiveness of China’s open-
source models. Initially the focus was on the cheapness of China models and the related 
commoditisation threat facing large language models. But now there is also a growing 
realisation that China has become a technological peer to the US in AI, as well as in so many 
other areas.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
The question now is whether all of the above has been priced fully into markets. This writer 
does not think so. It is also important to understand that the AI story also faces growing credit 
risks. For the AI capex spending has over the past year increasingly been funded less by 
hyperscalers’ cash and more by debt. Indeed, the hyperscalers are now the largest issuer by 
category of investment grade debt.  
The reason credit risk has become more of an issue is that it can no longer be assumed, most 
particularly with the arrival of the likes of Kimi K3, that large language models will ever be 
profitable given the related ongoing collapse in token pricing. This is extremely relevant for the 
hyperscalers since they have invested in data center capacity on the assumption that the 
compute will be purchased by the likes of Anthropic and OpenAI.  
In this respect, there is a 2008 real estate credit angle to this AI boom in the sense that the 
hyperscalers have invested in data centers on the assumption of a promise by the assumed 
tenants of future payment in exchange for future compute. The result is that, of around 
US$2.3tn in aggregate contracted backlog across the four big platforms (Microsoft, Alphabet, 
Amazon and Oracle), about half is owed by OpenAI and Anthropic.  
What is the form of these contractual commitments? The credit is extended in the specific form 
of so-called remaining performance obligations (RPOs). These RPOs totalled US$2.34tn at the 
end of 2Q26 and have grown by 186% or US$1.52tn over the past four quarters from US$816bn 
at the end of 2Q25 (see following chart). 
Hyperscalers' remaining performance obligations (RPOs) (contracted revenue backlog) 
  
Source: Bloomberg, Companies 
 
So far investors have been viewing the rising RPOs positively as evidence of demand to justify 
the hyperscaler capex and related data center build-out. But clearly there is a lot of scope for 
these assumptions to be questioned since the hyperscalers and others such as the neo-clouds 
like CoreWeave have in effect made concentrated unsecured loans to what, for now at least, 
are cash-burning tenants since neither Anthropic or OpenAI are profitable. In the case of the 
neo-clouds the risk is even greater since they have themselves borrowed to rent out the chips. 
This is why credit spreads in AI related borrowers have now to be monitored closely for any 
sign that investors are becoming unwilling to finance AI capex. Data center securitisation will 
be one important area to monitor.

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
So, the nature of the hyperscaler businesses has changed dramatically, as have their balance 
sheets. There is also the issue of the off-balance sheet data center lease commitments taken 
on by the hyperscalers. A Moody’s report in February highlighted that the top five US 
hyperscalers (including Oracle) had accumulated US$662bn in future data center lease 
commitments not yet commenced that are not current liabilities and therefore sit off their 
balance sheets, up from US$152bn at the end of 2023 (see Moody’s report: “Hyperscalers’ 
reported AI-related lease commitments may understate economic risk”, 23 February 2026). A 
more recent Nikkei study concluded that the five hyperscalers’ so-called “hidden debt” totaled 
US$1.65tn at the end of 2Q26 exceeding the roughly US$1.35tn in debt reported on their balance 
sheets (see Nikkei Asia article: “Five US tech giants’ hidden debts soar to US$1.65tn on opaque 
AI funding”, 21 July 2026). Under accounting rules, GPUs and servers under long-term contracts 
that have not yet been delivered, as well as lease agreements for data centers that are not yet 
operational, are treated as off-balance-sheet items. 
Still, as already noted, US earnings growth remains stellar for now. Indeed it is way above the 
average of 10% for the S&P500 recorded over the past ten years. But it is worth noting that 
earnings were similarly way above trend at the peak of the dot-com boom in early 2000. S&P500 
annualised EPS rose by 33% YoY in the four quarters to 1Q00, compared with an average of 8% 
in the previous ten years. It is further worth highlighting that the current trend in S&P500 
earnings looks out of line with the trend in macro profits released by the Bureau of Economic 
Analysis, as was also the case in 2000. Thus, S&P500 as reported EPS has risen by an 
annualised 18% over the past three years to 2Q26, while the National Accounts measure of 
nonfinancial corporate profits is up by only an annualised 5% over the same period. Similarly, 
S&P500 EPS rose by an annualised 10% in the three years to 3Q00, while the macro corporate 
profit measure was down an annualised 8% over the same period (see following chart). 
S&P500 as reported EPS vs NIPA nonfinancial corporate profits after tax (annualised) 
  
Note: Annualised data up to 2Q26. Log scale. Source: S&P Global, Bureau of Economic Analysis 
 
The above suggests financial engineering at work. One potential financial engineering aspect 
to recent hyperscalers’ profits is the role played by “other income and expenses” in boosting 
hyperscaler profits. This item captures non cash unrealised gains in investments, which is likely 
to include positions in unlisted AI-related stocks. 
Thus, the four major hyperscalers’ annualised earnings increased by US$222bn YoY to 
US$581bn in the four quarters to 2Q26, while “other non-operating income/loss” increased by 
US$224bn YoY to US$237bn over the same period, accounting for all of the increase in earnings 
(see following chart).

All of the above are reasons why both the relative and absolute return performance of the 
hyperscaler stocks should continue to be monitored closely. The bottom line for now is that it 
remains extremely unclear which if any of the hyperscalers will monetise their AI capex 
successfully. There is also the real issue of whether their legacy businesses which have funded 
the capex will be disrupted by AI. This is why the base case remains that the US made an all-
time peak as a percentage of world stock market capitalization on 24 December 2024. Thus, 
MSCI USA’s weighting in the MSCI AC World Index peaked at 67.2% on 24 December 2024 and 
closed last quarter at 64.2% (see following chart).  
MSCI USA as % of MSCI AC World 
  
Source: FactSet, MSCI  
 
None of the above is to say that AI is a non-story. In this respect, it is worth citing again Amara's 
Law first coined by American futurist Roy Amara in 1978. That says “we tend to overestimate 
the effect of a technology in the short run and underestimate the effect in the long run”. In this 
respect, the time for an extended AI hangover after the initial surge of enthusiasm is 
approaching. But in the long term the arrival of AI means that the demand for compute will keep 
growing helped by collapsing token prices based on the by now widely understood Jevons 
Paradox, first coined back in 1865 by British economist William Stanley Jevons. On this point, 
the decline in token prices is reflected in the Silicon Data’s LLM Token Expenditure Index which 
tracks the average price the market pays for a million AI tokens. This has declined by 52% since 
peaking in late May to US$0.98/m tokens (see following chart).

Meanwhile staying on the AI issue, there was an interesting, albeit strange, development 
towards the end of last quarter. This was Anthropic’s Dario Amodei, OpenAI’s Sam Altman and 
Elon Musk all publicly agreeing to hit the brakes as regards the development of AI at the so-
called “frontier” (see The Guardian article: “OpenAI boss and Elon Musk back calls to put brakes 
on ‘reckless’ AI development”, 13 September 2026).  
What is the rationale for such a move? The advice here is to look at the incentives. A 
coordinated pause sold as “safety” driven could be viewed as a way to build a regulatory moat, 
most particularly if there is a subsequent plan to ban open-source models, most particularly 
those from China. The other possibility, and it is not mutually exclusive, is that the AI titans have 
realized that the cost of compute is becoming unsustainable and/or that the demand for the 
models is starting to plateau. If that is the case, why not attempt to lock in a cartel now? Still it 
should also be noted that Meta Chairman Mark Zuckerberg in September rejected the calls for 
such an industry-wide slowdown. This came after the successful launch of Meta’s Muse on 8 
September, the most successful consumer AI application yet. Meta’s share price has risen by 
18% since the launch (see following chart).

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
Meanwhile, as regards the prime focus on data centers in terms of AI capex spending, it is also 
worth referencing an academic study conducted by a team from Stanford University which was 
first published last November and most recently revised on 6 September (“Intelligence per Watt: 
Measuring Intelligence Efficiency of Local AI” by Jon Saad-Falcon, Avanika Narayan and other 
researchers, Stanford University & Together AI, 6 September 2026). This study compared the 
performance of small language models (SLMs), which is the model which in this writer’s view 
will be increasingly adopted by corporates, with the performance of large language models 
(LLMs) run in data centers. The findings are that “SLMs” are catching up fast in terms of 
performance with energy and compute costs which are between 50% and 85% lower. Now it is 
true that LLMs are, for now at least, still way ahead in agentic AI applications. But that may not 
last forever while applications that can be run on personal computers are vastly cheaper as 
regards the cost of energy. The above also means there may be less need for expensive chips 
than previously assumed (i.e. CPUs rather than GPUs). 
The message from the Stanford study is that there is an incentive for companies to restructure 
AI budgets away from centralised “cloud endpoints” towards so-called “on premises” SLMs. 
While banks already have to be “on premises” for obvious data security reasons, it would seem 
that every company will also want to be “on-prem” for the same data security reasons, if they 
can afford it.  
None of this means that AI is not here to stay, nor that the demand for compute will keep rising. 
But it does mean that the AI story will be due for a big reset at some point in the not so distant 
future. Are the four hyperscalers really going to spend US$993bn on capex in 2027 which is the 
current estimate of the consensus? 
Meanwhile, if China still looks well positioned in AI, its economy remains characterised by 
subdued domestic demand even as its export sector continues to boom. The macro situation 
is discussed in more detail in the China country section on page 62.  
China is also preparing for a changing world order by its growing focus on gold. Recent 
developments on this front are worth highlighting. The People’s Bank of China reported an 
increase of 20.2 tonnes in its gold reserves to 2,387 tonnes at the end of August, the biggest 
monthly increase since October 2023. China’s official gold holdings have now risen for 22 
consecutive months with an accumulated increase of 122.2 tonnes. China’s gold reserves now 
account for 9.1% of its total foreign reserves (see following charts). 
China monthly increase in gold reserves 
  
Source: PBOC

Further evidence of rising demand for bullion comes from Chinese import data. Gold imports 
rose by 64% YoY to 934 tonnes in the six months to August, the highest level since the six 
months to May 2024 (see following chart). While in financial terms gold imports totaled 
US$135bn in the six months to August in the context of a Chinese trade surplus of US$596bn 
over the same period.  
China gold imports in tonnes and US dollar 
  
Note: Data up to August 2026. Source: General Administration of Customs, Bloomberg 
 
A Bloomberg article attributed one of the reasons for the demand for bullion to commercial 
banks building up their inventories to provide the physical backing for retail bullion sales and 
so-called “gold accumulation accounts”, where Chinese people buy physical gold every month 
or quarter on an ongoing basis often in small increments (see Bloomberg article: “China’s Gold 
Imports Surge After International Prices Slump”, 24 July 2026). While there is no official data on 
the size of gold accumulation accounts, Jefferies’ China financial analyst Betty Li estimates 
that the amount could be around Rmb200-300bn, similar to the China gold ETF assets under 
management which currently total Rmb276bn, up from only Rmb52bn in July 2024 (see 
following chart).

China gold ETFs' total assets

This is in the context of a Chinese government which, judging by its recent actions, appears to 
be actively discouraging retail investor trading in paper gold. Thus, the Industrial and 
Commercial Bank of China (ICBC) announced in late June that it was ending individual precious 
metals trading linked to the Shanghai Gold Exchange (SGE), effective 24 July. Both leveraged 
deferred contracts and spot contracts were discontinued, while retail investors have to close 
positions or take delivery by that deadline. This follows similar announcements made by other 
banks. 
Meanwhile it should be noted that the Shanghai International Gold Exchange (SGEI), set up in 
September 2014 and fully owned by SGE, is built entirely around physical convertibility where 
contracts can only be settled with physical bullion stored in certified vaults. This enables foreign 
institutions and individuals to have access to the China precious metals market. 
Interestingly, there have been two other related developments of late. The first was the launch 
on 7 July by the Hong Kong Precious Metals Central Clearing Company (HKPMCC) of a central 
gold clearing and settlement system in Hong Kong which features a new Hong Kong-specific 
gold price reference rate HAU. It also introduced the initial phase of the so-called “Delivery 
Connect” in partnership with the Shanghai Gold Exchange to enable cross-border settlement 
using physical gold assets. This should be viewed as the equivalent for gold of Hong Kong’s 
successful Stock and Bond Connects which have been operating since November 2014 and 
July 2017, respectively. 
Second, there was the opening in June last year in Hong Kong of a Shanghai International Gold 
Exchange-owned certified gold vault. This is the first of a series of planned offshore gold vaults 
with plans to open similar vaults in Singapore and Riyadh. In a further related development the 
Hong Kong Government announced last September plans to expand its overall gold storage 
capacity from 200 tonnes to more than 2,000 tonnes within three years. By contrast, a paper 
market needs no vaults since gold is traded in paper contracts, as is the case in the London 
bullion market or New York’s Comex. 
How to interpret all of the above? It would seem to be the case that China is actively 
encouraging a move to trading and investing in physical gold, away from what a gold bug purist 
would regard as paper gold shenanigans. This is because the whole appeal of gold as a store 
of value hedge is that it is not part of the credit system; that it is an IOU nothing. 
If China succeeds in moving the focus in the gold market from paper to physical, in a global 
macro-economic context where the US dollar debasement trade kicks in again on evidence of 
policies of financial suppression such as yield curve control, the long-term end game should be

Asia Maxima 
Global and regional overview 
that paper gold trades at a discount to the physical. But the question by then will be whether 
Western governments will still allow the physical ownership of gold by residents in their 
jurisdictions.   
Meanwhile, it is worth recalling that the original focus of setting up the Shanghai Gold Exchange 
was probably primarily related to China’s desire to establish an infrastructure for settling trade 
outside the US dollar system. In this respect, gold is one way of settling trade if a party trading 
with China, such as a seller of oil or other commodities who is receiving Chinese currency, ends 
up with excess renminbi at the end of any given period. The other part of that infrastructure was 
the People’s Bank of China constructing an alternative clearing and settlement system outside 
the US dollar. The Cross-Broder Interbank Payment System, known as CIPS, was set up in 2015 
and has been growing ever since. CIPS transaction value has risen from Rmb481bn in 2015 to 
Rmb180tn in 2025 and Rmb140tn in the first eight months of 2026 (see following chart).  
Like so much else as regards the mainland, all this has been done incrementally. China has 
never formally challenged the US dollar paper standard in terms of an open confrontation. Still 
it is worth recalling again that the then PBOC Governor Zhou Xiaochuan wrote an article for the 
BIS in 2009 where he argued that America had abused its position of printing the reserve 
currency of the world and called for a reform of the international currency system based on the 
IMF’s SDRs (see BIS article: “Reform the international monetary system” by Zhou Xiaochuan, 23 
March 2009). This article was written in the aftermath of the global financial crisis which had 
its origins in hyper leveraged securitisation created and sold in America.  
China CIPS transaction value 
  
Source: Cross-Border Interbank Payments System (CIPS)

Asia asset allocation 
Asia asset allocation 
Wall Street-correlated world stock markets mostly traded sideways last quarter supported by 
robust earnings growth but under pressure from rising long-term bond yields. Renewed 
geopolitical tensions also remained a wild card. 
MSCI America rose by 2.1% last quarter, while MSCI AC World increased by 1.3%. So America 
was a marginal outperformer. Year to date it is a marginal underperformer, rising by 11.7% 
compared with a 11.8% gain for MSCI AC World in US dollar terms. 
As for MSCI Europe, it declined by 2% in US dollar terms last quarter and therefore 
underperformed. It has also underperformed year to date, rising by 3.8%, a performance which 
incorporates a 3.5% depreciation of the euro against the US dollar year to date. However, Japan 
was an outperformer. MSCI Japan rose by 5.2% in US dollar terms last quarter. It is also an 
outperformer year to date rising by 20.7%, a performance which incorporates a 0.4% 
depreciation of the yen against the US dollar. 
MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex-Japan underperformed last quarter declining by 0.8% reflecting the 
correction in AI-geared semiconductor stocks in Korea and Taiwan. Still it is still a major 
outperformer year to date rising by 21.8%. The same applies to MSCI Emerging Markets which 
declined by 1.0% last quarter but is again an outperformer year to date rising by 21.4%. Both 
indices also outperformed last year, rising by 26.8% and 30.6% respectively, which was the best 
outperformance for both asset classes, relative to MSCI AC World, since 2017. 
The best guide to valuation remains the trailing price-to-book (PB) ratio. The MSCI AC Asia 
Pacific ex-Japan’s trailing PB is now 2.45x compared with a mean of 1.86x since 1995. The 
index is now trading at 15.4x trailing PE, based on companies with positive earnings. This 
compares with the trailing mean valuation of 14.2x since 1995. On a forward earnings basis, 
the MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex-Japan universe is now trading at 12.4x 2026 earnings based on 
consensus forecast earnings growth of 68% in 2026.

Asia Maxima 
Asia asset allocation 
An Overweight continues to be recommended on Asia ex-Japan equities. The long term view 
remains that the US dollar has made a major peak which is a positive for Asia and emerging 
markets. Still the US dollar declined against Asian currencies last quarter despite rising Fed 
tightening expectations. It has declined by 2.0% last quarter and 1.2% year-to-date against the 
Asia Dollar Index, which includes nine major Asia ex-Japan currencies, after declining by 3.2% 
last year. 
MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex-Japan trailing price-to-book (PB) 
 
Source: Bloomberg, Jefferies 
 
Asia continues to offer certain defensive qualities in terms of still-significant dividend support. 
The MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex-Japan universe has a projected 2026 dividend payout ratio of 28%. 
A total of 13% of the stocks in the universe have a forecast 2026 dividend yield higher than the 
US 10-year Treasury bond yield of 5.28% and 42% have a forecast dividend yield higher than 
their local 10-year government bond yields. A key factor here was the rise in Treasury bond 
yields last quarter.

Asia Maxima 
Asia asset allocation 
Japan remains an Overweight supported by a cheap currency and the rebound in nominal GDP 
growth. There is also the ongoing evidence of a change in capital management in terms of 
rising share buybacks and the like. The Topix’s forecast dividend payout ratio is projected at 
36% in 2026, up from 17% in 2004, while the forecast ROE for the MSCI Japan universe is 12% 
in 2026, up from 3% in 2009.  
Still valuations are no longer cheap. The Topix is trading at 1.83x trailing book compared with 
a long-term average since 1994 of 1.52x. MSCI Japan is trading at 17.4x trailing PE, based on 
companies with positive earnings, versus an average of 19.9x since 1997. On a forward 
earnings basis, MSCI Japan is trading at 16.6x 2026 forecast earnings, assuming 27% earnings 
growth in 2026.

As for the asset allocation within the Asia Pacific ex-Japan relative-return portfolio, Australia 
marginally outperformed last quarter, rising by 0.4% in US dollar terms compared with a 0.8% 
decline in the MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex-Japan benchmark. It is an underperformer year to date 
rising by 7.1% compared with a 21.8% gain in the regional benchmark. This is after it 
underperformed last year, rising by 10.9% compared with a 26.8% gain in the regional 
benchmark. The performance incorporates a 4.1% appreciation of the Australian dollar against 
the US dollar year to date. 
The Underweight in Australia was maintained last quarter because of expensive valuations, 
most particularly in the banking sector. The resources sector continues to be favoured over the 
banking sector.

Asia Maxima 
Asia asset allocation 
As for the asset allocation in the rest of the Asia Pacific ex-Japan relative-return portfolio, Korea 
underperformed last quarter declining by 10.1% in US dollar terms with the sell-off in 
semiconductor stocks. But it remains the major outperformer year to date, rising by 95.5% in 
US dollar terms. This follows its 96.6% gain last year, which was its best relative 
outperformance in a regional context since 1998. The performance incorporates a 6.1% 
appreciation of the won against the US dollar year-to-date as the won appreciated by 14.2% last 
quarter. 
Korea was maintained as an Underweight last quarter. Korea’s weighting in the MSCI AC Asia 
Pacific ex-Japan benchmark has risen from 8.6% at the end of 2024 to a peak of 24.6% in late 
June and ended last quarter at 21.1%. 
India again underperformed last quarter, declining by 5.9% in US dollar terms. It is also an 
underperformer year to date declining by 15.6%, a performance which incorporates a further 
6.2% depreciation of the rupee against the US dollar year to date after a 4.7% depreciation last 
year. 
India was maintained as a slight Overweight last quarter on evidence of a cyclical rebound in 
domestic demand. The Neutral weighting has declined from 18.6% at the end of 2024 to 10.5%. 
Still even after the recent underperformance, India has outperformed the Asia Pacific ex-Japan 
benchmark by 17% since April 2020. However, valuations are still at a premium to the rest of 
the region. 
Asia Pacific ex-Japan asset allocation 
 
Source: MSCI, Jefferies 
 
MSCI Hong Kong outperformed last quarter rising by 5.4% in US dollar terms. Still it remains an 
underperformer year to date rising by 2.1%. Hong Kong was maintained as an Overweight last 
quarter. The main positive is that the residential property market has bottomed amid growing 
evidence of a domestic recovery as well as decent dividend support. 
Thailand underperformed last quarter, declining by 5.8% in US dollar terms. It is also now an 
underperformer year to date rising by 14.6%. This performance incorporates a 6.1% 
depreciation of the baht against the US dollar year to date. The longstanding Underweight 
became a Neutral in the second half of last year because of a lower benchmark weighting and 
is now a marginal Overweight. The positive is renewed political stability and a slight 
improvement in the long running neo-deflationary macro environment.

Asia Maxima 
Asia asset allocation 
Malaysia marginally outperformed last quarter, rising by 0.4% in US dollar terms. But it remains 
an underperformer year to date declining by 1.0%. The performance incorporates a 0.5% 
depreciation of the ringgit against the US dollar after a 10.1% appreciation last year when it was 
Asia’s strongest currency. 
Malaysia was maintained as a marginal Overweight last quarter. The positives are a solid 
growth outlook and for now continuing political stability. The market remains cheap relative to 
historic valuations. 
Singapore outperformed last quarter, rising by 12.1% in US dollar terms. Still it remains a 
marginal underperformer year to date rising by 19.7%, a performance which incorporates a 0.6% 
appreciation of the Singapore dollar against the US dollar.   
Singapore was maintained as a Neutral but it became a slight Underweight at the end of last 
quarter as a result of the outperformance. Credit growth has remained positive, while a further 
positive is a forecast 2026 dividend yield for MSCI Singapore of 3.5% in the context of Asia’s 
fundamentally strongest currency. 
Indonesia outperformed last quarter rising by 2.5% in US dollar terms.  But it remains the worst 
performer year to date declining by 42.3% in US dollar terms. This performance incorporates a 
6.6% depreciation of the rupiah against the US dollar year to date though it appreciated by 0.1% 
last quarter.   
Indonesia was maintained as an Overweight primarily because the Neutral weighting has kept 
declining. It has fallen from 1.4% at the start of 2025 to only 0.4%. The stock market is at 
relatively depressed valuations but has suffered from escalating investor concerns as regards 
the populist policies of the current government. Meanwhile, both the central bank governor and 
the finance minister were replaced last quarter. 
MSCI Asia country performance in US dollar terms (3Q26)  
 
Source: Datastream 
 
The Philippines underperformed last quarter declining by 5.6% in US dollar terms. It is also an 
underperformer year to date declining by 6.2%, a performance which incorporates a 6.1% 
depreciation of the peso against the US dollar year to date. 
The Philippines is maintained as an Overweight in the context of a Neutral weighting of only 
0.2%, down from 0.5% at the start of 2025. It is a consumption-driven economy where 
valuations remain extremely depressed by historical standards but where a lack of energy 
subsidies has made the consumer extremely vulnerable. The other risk is the currency in the 
context of the country’s twin deficits, which is why a renewed weakening in the US dollar would 
be a relief.

Taiwan again outperformed last quarter rising by 4.2% in US dollar terms. Taiwan is also a major 
outperformer year to date rising by 68.4%, a performance which incorporated a 1.3% 
depreciation of the NT dollar against the US dollar year to date.  
Taiwan was maintained as an Underweight last quarter because of the extent of the previous 
outperformance based on the boom triggered by the now more than three-and-a-half year old 
AI capex arms race. The Neutral weighting has increased from 12.9% to 28.6% since the start 
of 2023. 
Lastly China, in the sense of the MSCI China Index, which now includes A shares and Hong 
Kong- and US-quoted China stocks, outperformed last quarter, rising by 2.9% in US dollar terms. 
But it remains an underperformer year to date declining by 13.4%. This is after it outperformed 
last year, rising by 28.0% in US dollar terms. This performance incorporates a 4.2% appreciation 
of the renminbi against the US dollar year to date following a 4.5% appreciation last year. 
China was maintained as an Overweight last quarter. The Neutral weighting has increased from 
18.8% at the end of 2Q26 to 19.5%. Valuations were 8.0x 12-month forward earnings at the 
bottom in January 2024 and are now 10.2x on a 12-month forward earnings basis. Meanwhile, 
the domestic A share market, as measured by the CSI 300 Index, again diverged from MSCI 
China declining by 11.4% last quarter in US dollar terms. It is now down 1.9% year to date. As a 
result, Hong Kong-quoted H shares ended last quarter trading at a 19.7% discount to their A 
share counterparts.

An out-of-the-index allocation in Vietnam was re-introduced in 2Q18. The Vietnam Stock Index 
declined by 3.6% last quarter in US dollar terms, and so underperformed. It is up 0.4% year to 
date. This is after it was up 36.5% last year, making it an outperformer in 2025. The dong 
appreciated by 1.3% against the US dollar year to date.  
Vietnam is now up 50.8% in US dollar terms since inclusion in mid-May 2018, compared with a 
54.6% gain in the regional benchmark index over the same period. The allocation to Vietnam 
was maintained last quarter. 
Vietnam Stock Index 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
The above asset allocation is for relative-return investors. The regional thematic Asia ex-Japan 
long-only absolute-return portfolio, free from the tyranny of benchmarks, is shown in the 
following table. This portfolio is not trying to outperform a benchmark but simply attempting to 
make money, though it is important to note that the portfolio has not been allowed to own cash 
since its inception at the end of 3Q02. This naturally limits the options considerably when 
regional markets decline. 
The long-term performance of the Asia ex-Japan thematic portfolio remains, for now, relatively 
satisfactory. Since its inception at the end of 3Q02, the portfolio has risen on a total-return basis 
by 4,792% in US-dollar terms, compared with a 1,128% increase in the MSCI AC Asia ex-Japan 
Index and a 1,379% increase in the S&P500 (see following chart). This means the portfolio has 
risen by an annualised 17.6% since inception, compared with an annualised 11.0% increase in 
the MSCI AC Asia ex-Japan Index and an annualised 11.9% gain in the S&P500.

sectors;  Investment Horizon: Aim is to deliver outperformance over one-year time-frame;  Investment Objective: The aim is to achieve long-term 
absolute-return performance;  Launch date: 30 September 2002; Updated on 10 September 2026. The portfolio undergoes periodical review linked 
to either macro events (such as government budget, major central bank policy changes etc.) and stock specific events (analyst reco change, price 
movement etc.); Risks: OWT stance on certain stocks can impact portfolio performance if these stocks UPF, and vice versa. 
  
The portfolio outperformed last quarter rising by 3.2% in US dollar terms on a total-return basis, 
compared with a 0.2% decline in the MSCI AC Asia ex-Japan Index. But it remains an 
underperformer year to date rising by 13.6%, compared with a 26.0% gain in the regional 
benchmark, reflecting the underweight in the major semiconductor stocks. Three stocks, TSMC, 
Samsung Electronics and Hynix still account for 33.5% of the MSCI Asia ex-Japan benchmark 
though down from 35.4% at the end of 2Q26.  
The portfolio ended last quarter 20% invested in India, way down from a peak of 50% in mid-
February 2024, and 40% invested in China/Hong Kong. The portfolio also has 26% invested in 
Taiwan and Korea. It should be noted again that the stated performance of the absolute-return 
portfolio is on a total-return basis.

Moving away from regionally orientated equity portfolios, a global long-only equity portfolio was 
introduced on 19 January 2023, or four days before the announcement of the Microsoft 
investment in ChatGPT-maker OpenAI which served as the catalyst for the AI narrative to drive 
Wall Street-correlated world stock markets. The portfolio is shown in the following table. 
This timing proved an initial negative for the portfolio, which began with a 66% weighting in Asia 
Pacific, of which 23% comprised India. The portfolio outperformed last quarter, rising by 5.3% 
in US dollar terms compared with a 1.7% rise in MSCI AC World. But it remains an 
underperformer year to date, rising by 10.3% compared with a 13.4% gain in the benchmark. 
The portfolio is now up 99.6% since inception in US dollar terms on a total -return basis 
compared with a 94.6% gain in MSCI AC World (see following chart).

A global sovereign bond portfolio was launched on 26 March 2020, consisting initially only of 
Asian government bonds, to highlight the point that Asian and other emerging market local 
currency government bonds made a better long-term investment than G7 government bonds 
given the ultra-low, if not negative, bond yields prevailing in the G7 universe at that time. The 
portfolio is shown below. It has an average yield to maturity of 6.97%. By contrast, the 
Bloomberg Barclays G7 government bond index yields 4.41%, up from 0.35% at the time of the 
portfolio’s inception.

following a 154.6% gain last year. This was its best annual performance ever since the index

Gold is viewed as in a healthy consolidation following last year’s big gains. Historically, gold’s 
performance in US dollar terms has been negatively correlated with the level of real interest 
rates, or the lack of them. This is evident from the two charts below, though in recent years that 
correlation has weakened significantly following record central bank buying of gold since 3Q22, 
triggered by the reaction to the US decision in late February 2022 to freeze Russia’s foreign 
exchange reserves. As a result, global central banks bought a record net 1,080 tonnes of gold 
in 2022, 1,051 tonnes in 2023, 1,086 tonnes in 2024 and 848 tonnes in 2025 (see following 
chart). They have continued to buy this year, buying a net 345 tonnes in 1H26.  
The gold price should remain ultra-sensitive to any further changes in Fed language or 
messaging, as well as still escalating geopolitical risks. In this respect, the more hawkish 
messaging of Fed chairman Kevin Warsh last quarter poses a near term risk, though for now 
the Fed balance sheet is expanding again. Meanwhile, the gold-mining index has outperformed 
gold bullion by 82.1% since its recent bottom on 28 February 2024 when the gold price was 
US$2,034/oz.

Global central banks’ net purchases of gold

The positive long-term case for gold remains that the Fed will not be able to normalise monetary 
policy properly. If this became more evident with the dramatic MMT-lite policies announced by 
the Fed and other G7 central banks in 2Q20 to combat the collateral damage to the economy 
caused by lockdowns triggered by the pandemic, the Fed implemented since March 2022 the 
most aggressive monetary tightening in terms of nominal rate hikes since the early 1980s. Still, 
that monetary tightening cycle ended with renewed Fed easing since September 2024, while 
the Fed also ended in 4Q25 quantitative tightening in terms of its latest effort to shrink its 
balance sheet. 
The more hawkish message conveyed by Kevin Warsh last quarter, and the commencement of 
a renewed Fed tightening cycle, poses a near term risk though for now at least the Fed balance 
sheet is expanding again.  
A long-term bullish view is maintained on gold bullion, with the price target now US$6,600/oz. 
This has been derived by comparing the January 1980 peak gold price of US$850/oz with the 
increase in US nominal personal disposable income per capita since then. The gold price was 
then equivalent to 9.9% of US disposable income per capita which was US$8,551. The gold price 
is now US$4,157/oz or 5.9% of US disposable income per capita of US$70,620 as of August 
(see following chart). To reach 9.9% of US disposable income per capita means gold should 
rise to US$7,020/oz.

Asia Maxima 
Asia asset allocation 
The long-term view here remains the same as it has been for many years. That is that G7 central 
banks, including most importantly the Federal Reserve, will not be able to exit from 
unconventional monetary policy in a benign manner and will ultimately remain committed to 
ongoing central bank balance sheet expansion in one form or another. This is despite recent 
efforts by the Fed to shrink its balance sheet. The Fed balance sheet ended last quarter at 
US$6.74tn, down from a peak of US$8.97trn in April 2022 but far above the US$900bn level in 
mid-2008 before former Fed Chair Ben Bernanke launched quantitative easing in November 
2008 (see following chart). 
Federal Reserve balance sheet 
  
Note: Data up to the week ended 30 September 2026. Source: Federal Reserve 
 
It is believed such debasement policies will increasingly discredit those central banks that have 
pursued unconventional monetary policy, threatening the stability and indeed integrity of the 
current fiat paper-money system. Gold-mining stocks remain the geared way of investing on 
such a view. 
The above asset allocation means that this portfolio is still set up for a long-term US dollar 
debasement trade, as it has been since its initial launch at the end of 3Q02 when the gold price 
was US$323/oz — which could also be a fiat-currency debasement trade, given the subsequent 
resort to aggressive quantitative easing extending beyond the US, most notably in Japan and 
the Eurozone, and indeed given the growing resort to various forms of yield curve control, be 
they explicit or implicit. 
The fundamental view here remains that it is much easier to enter unorthodox monetary policy 
regimes than to exit them. Indeed, the longer-term risk remains that unorthodox monetary 
policy spreads from the developed world to the emerging world. Still, importantly, it should be 
noted that Chinese monetary policy remains, for now, orthodox. 
Such a failure to exit from unorthodox monetary policy in a benign manner is likely to culminate 
in the collapse of the US dollar paper standard to the benefit of gold bullion owners. It should 
also be emphasised again that the investment in gold is viewed as insurance, not as a short-
term trade. This is a long-term portfolio, which seeks to balance the long-term risks and 
opportunities in the current global context.

Asia Maxima 
Japan: The continuing need for normalisation 
Japan: The continuing need for normalisation 
The Bank of Japan failed to raise interest rates by more than the widely anticipated 25bp at its 
September meeting with the uncollateralized overnight call rate now at 1.25%. As a result, the 
yen sold off on the news and closed last quarter near the critical 160 level at Y157.4/US$.  
This highlights the growing risk that the market will increasingly focus on the fact that the 
Japanese central bank is moving so cautiously because it is worried about the government’s 
growing cost of debt servicing as a result of rising bond yields. Japan’s interest payments on 
government bonds have risen from Y7.1tn in FY22 ended 31 March 2023 to an estimated Y13tn 
this fiscal year and Y16.6tn next year beginning 1 April 2027, based on the latest FY27 budget 
request announced in late August. The Ministry of Finance has set the assumed interest rate 
on long-term government bonds, used to calculate debt servicing costs, at 3.8% for the FY27 
budget request, up from 3.0% in this fiscal year’s budget.   
This continuing reluctance on the part of the BoJ to get ahead of the market in terms of rate 
hike expectations should also be seen in the context of estimates of the neutral rate which are 
now at 2%. For such reasons another 25bp hike to 1.5% is expected in either October or 
December, while it is quite likely that the uncollateralized overnight call rate will reach 2% if not 
higher in this monetary tightening cycle.  
There is certainly plenty of data to justify such a move. Inflation (core CPI excl. fresh food) has 
been above 2% YoY since April 2022 though it has fallen below 2% this year primarily due to 
government subsidies for electricity and gas bills. Core CPI excluding fresh food rose by 1.7% 
YoY in August while CPI excluding fresh food and energy rose by 1.9% YoY. The BoJ’s new so-
called underlying inflation, which excludes so-called “institutional factors” defined as 
government measures with a one-off effect on inflation, has mostly been above 2% since 
February 2022 and was 2.6% YoY in August (2.2% excluding fresh food and energy). 
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
A further indication of rising price pressures is the corporate goods price index, which tracks 
prices of goods traded among companies. It rose by 7.6% YoY in August and has now exceeded 
7% for three consecutive months. This reflects the dual impact of higher crude oil prices and a 
weak yen which companies are increasingly expected to pass on to consumers. The yen-based 
import price index rose by 24.8% YoY in August, though down from a recent high of 30.6% YoY 
in June. 
This is why the weak yen, the consequence of years of ultra easy monetary policy, is a 
continuing negative both economically and politically since it is squeezing ordinary people. Yet 
the word is that Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi does not understand monetary policy, while 
Japan’s Growth Strategy Minister Minoru Kiuchi has a very pro reflationary mindset. Yet such
