Asia Maxima 
Japan: The continuing need for normalisation 
an outlook looks increasingly yesterday’s story as captured by major banks’ loan growth 
running at 7.9% YoY in August and nominal GDP growth expanding at a trend rate of 4.2% YoY 
in the past three years. 
All this highlights the key risk that BoJ Governor Kazuo Ueda has been running by remaining so 
cautious in terms of the normalisation of monetary policy. This is reflected in Japan’s 
steepening yield curve. The 10-year JGB yield rose to a 30-year high of 3.125% on 25 September 
and ended last quarter at 3.07%.  
Meanwhile, the Takaichi government has sought to shelter consumers from the impact of the 
cheap yen by focusing on the symptoms. There are two main policies. The first has been an 
increase in energy subsidies even as Japan has been importing more expensive oil. Data from 
the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry (METI) shows that Japan’s crude oil imports rose 
by 13% YoY to 77.7m barrels in August, with imports from the Middle East declining by 28% YoY 
to 47.1m barrels accounting for only 61% of total crude oil imports. This compared with an 
average market share of 93% in the 12 months to March. By contrast, crude oil imports from 
North America surged by 1,152% YoY to 26.7m barrels in August, accounting for 34% of the 
total, up from only 4.8% in the 12 months to March. 
The second policy is the proposal to reduce the 8% sales tax on food and nonalcoholic 
beverages to 1% for two years starting next April. The government will also introduce benefits 
for low- and middle-income earners, which will make the sales tax effectively zero. This is due 
to be voted on in the Diet during an extraordinary session due to be convened on 5 October. The 
tax cut will cost an estimated Y5tn per year. Meanwhile, defence-related spending for this year 
is projected to rise to Y10.6tn or 1.5% of GDP in the latest budget, based on current projected 
2026 GDP, up from 1.0% in 2022. 
Takaichi’s continuing preference for expansionary fiscal policy further highlights the need for 
monetary tightening. Meanwhile, another feature of her administration is industrial policy 
targeting 17 industries to promote domestic investment. The government unveiled the new 
growth strategy plan in late June, which was subsequently approved by the cabinet in July. The 
policy sets a target of over Y370tn (US$2.3tn) in combined public and private investment by 
fiscal 2040 in 17 strategic sectors, including AI and semiconductors. But the government has 
yet to disclose how much of the investment will come from the public sector. 
While such dirigiste policies are fashionable again globally, healthy Japanese companies will 
not want to take government money because of the resulting obligations. They also do not need 
to. Having been cashed up for years, with Topix-listed companies’ aggregate cash balance 
peaking at Y172tn at the end of March, capex has been growing at a healthy rate of late. 
Borrowing rates are comfortably lower than corporates’ rising expectations of where inflation 
will be in five years. Private non-residential investment has risen by an annualised 5% in nominal 
terms and 1.2% in real terms over the past three years, while annualised nominal private non-
residential investment as a percentage of GDP has increased from 17.4% in 2Q21 to 19% in 
2Q26. The average bank lending rate for new long-term loans was 1.95% for the three months 
to August, while the BoJ’s quarterly Tankan survey shows that enterprises’ inflation outlook five 
years ahead rose to 2.6% in 2Q26.  
Finally, the MSCI Japan is trading on 15.6 times 12-month forward earnings assuming 27% 
consensus earnings growth for this fiscal year ending 31 March 2027 and 11% in FY27.

Asia Maxima 
Australia: Downturn coming 
Australia: Downturn coming   
The risk of an economic downturn continues to increase. This follows 75bp of monetary 
tightening in the first half of this year with the Reserve Bank of Australia (RBA) raising rates by 
another 25bp to 4.6% at its meeting in late September. This tightening is in the context of 13 
months of inflation data running above the RBA’s target range of 2-3%. CPI inflation rose from 
a low of 1.9% YoY in June 2025 to 4.6% YoY in March and was 4.0% YoY in August.   
The latest hike came after RBA Governor Michele Bullock told a parliamentary committee in 
September that inflation risks were “skewed to the upside”. Still money markets expect no more 
tightening for the rest of this year but another 25bp hike in 1H27. Still more monetary tightening 
is not a given is because there is growing evidence of weakness in the residential property 
market. Home prices in Australia fell for a sixth month in a row in September according to 
property consultant Cotality. Sydney home prices have now declined by 8.6% over the past 
seven months and Melbourne prices by 6.9%. The aggregate impact of higher interest rates on 
Australia’s current A$2.6tn residential mortgage book is an estimated increase of about 
A$26bn in debt servicing payments based on the 100bp of rate hikes so far this year.  
As discussed in this section last quarter, the budget announced in May by the Labor government 
has further increased the risk of a housing downturn with two dramatic changes. First, from 
July 2027 capital gains at the marginal tax rate will be levied on capital gains on all assets, not 
just property. This is 47% for a higher income earner. Uptil now the capital gains tax was only 
levied at half the marginal rate. Second, the longstanding tax deduction on negative gearing will 
be exempted on all new property purchases save for a newly completed property. The latter 
exemption is designed to encourage construction given the shortage of housing.  
Still the reality is that the changes, which passed parliament in late June as expected, will likely 
lead to a significant decline in property transactions. First, homeowners will not want to 
crystalize the capital gains tax. Second, the negative gearing deduction is maintained, 
importantly, for existing properties. National home sales declined by 20.9% YoY in September 
and were down 17.4% YoY in the three months to September, according to Cotality estimates.   
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
If the budget was sold politically by the incumbent Labor government as a way of addressing 
housing affordability, it has backfired badly judging by the opinion polls. The 
Australian/Newspoll surveys show that Labor’s support has declined from 31% in the pre-budget 
poll in April to 27% in September while the anti-immigration One Nation party’s support rose 
from 24% to 30% over the same period. Still the next federal election is not due until May 2028. 
Meanwhile housing affordability was already an issue prior to this year’s rate hikes. The median 
dwelling value of A$860,529 was 8.2 times median annual pre-tax household income of 
A$104,390 as of September last year, according to Cotality’s annual Housing Affordability 
Report published last November. This is in the context of a then near record level of household

Asia Maxima 
Australia: Downturn coming 
income (45%) required to service a mortgage. The dwelling value to income ratio has since risen 
to 8.7x at the end of June with the percentage of income to service a mortgage rising to 51.1%. 
It is also the case that about 70% of mortgage debt remains linked to variable rates. The 
variable-rate mortgage rate for owner occupiers is currently 6.24%.  
Meanwhile, investor activity has remained strong of late with lending to investors still 
comprising 42% of mortgage demand by value in New South Wales in 2Q26, though down from 
an eight-year high of 46.2% in 3Q25, according to the Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS).  But 
investor activity should now collapse with the longstanding negative gearing tax deduction on 
purchases of new investment properties removed.  
Australia’s core macro issue remains the lack of productivity growth in an economy 
characterized by heavy regulation and structurally high wage growth where the public sector is 
the main driver of employment. Real GDP per hour worked declined by 0.2% YoY in 2Q26 and is 
down 5.0% since peaking in 1Q22. The safety valve remains continuing immigration with net 
overseas migration still running at 292,137 in the four quarters to March 2026. The importance 
of immigration in boosting growth is reflected in the fact that real GDP per capita was up only 
0.7% YoY in 2Q26 and was still 0.4% below the peak reached in 4Q22. 
The latest monetary tightening cycle began in the context of a cyclical pickup. But growth has 
started to turn down again. Real GDP grew by 2.6% YoY in 4Q25, the strongest YoY growth in 
11 quarters; but it slowed to 2.1% YoY in 2Q26. Meanwhile, the unemployment rate rose to 4.6% 
in August, the highest level since November 2021. Still system credit growth has for now held 
up rising by 8.6% YoY in June and 8.4% YoY in August, though it now seems likely to slow 
further. Investor housing loan growth has already slowed from a recent high of 10.4% YoY in 
June to 9.6% YoY in August.  
The key driver for the stock market remains the flows from Australia’s compulsory 
superannuation savings schemes, now running at A$236bn a year. The flows from the “super 
funds”, combined with a dividend “franking” scheme which in practice allows domestic 
shareholders to receive dividends tax free, remain the main reasons why the stock market 
trades at a multiple higher than justified by the level of earnings growth. MSCI Australia is on 
18.4x FY6/27 earnings based on forecast earnings growth of 4.2%, while it has a forecast 
dividend payout ratio of 66%.  
As regards the performance of the two main sectors, the resources sector has risen by 20% 
year-to-date on a total-return basis while the banks are down 2.6%. The banks now have a 30.7% 
weighting in the MSCI Australia Index and the resources sector ex-gold 26.8%. After a lengthy 
period of relative underperformance, the ASX200 Resources Index has outperformed the 
ASX200 Banks Index by 66% since June 2025, after underperforming by 51% since June 2023.

Asia Maxima 
China: A lack of reflation 
China: A lack of reflation   
There has been no evidence of a revival in animal spirits in terms of domestic consumption. 
The latest credit and macro data in China shows a continuing slowdown in activity. Renminbi 
bank loan growth slowed from 5.1% YoY in July to 4.9% YoY in August while, more importantly, 
household loans declined by 1.6% YoY in August with a record decline of Rmb1.03tn recorded 
in the first eight months of this year. As for consumption, nominal retail sales growth slowed to 
0.4% YoY in August. For such reasons it is not surprising that the Chinese government bond 
market remains well supported. The ten-year government bond yield closed last quarter at 
1.68%, only 9bp above the all-time low of 1.59% reached on 3 January 2025. 
So the Chinese government bond market is not confirming the reflationary trend in terms of the 
moves of PPI and CPI in recent months into positive territory. PPI and CPI rose by 3.8% YoY and 
0.8% YoY respectively in August. Meanwhile, aggressive stimulus is not expected beyond 
incremental fiscal easing in the second half of this year. On this point, the Politburo at its 30 
July meeting called for “accelerating the pace of fiscal expenditure and bond fund utilisation”. 
While the State Council executive meeting on 28 September, chaired by Premier Li Qiang, also 
stated that “efforts should be made to intensify counter-cyclical adjustment” and that “the 
issuance and utilisation of various types of bonds will be accelerated”. China has issued 
Rmb7.8tn of local government bonds in the first eight months of this year, compared with 
Rmb7.68tn in the same period last year.   
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
Still, it is worth noting another interesting development in the latest macro data. Nominal GDP 
growth exceeded real GDP growth in 2Q26 for the first time in 13 quarters. Nominal GDP growth 
rose from 4.9% YoY in 1Q26 to 5.9% YoY in 2Q26, while real GDP growth slowed from 5.0% YoY 
to 4.3% YoY over the same period.  
This development should be a positive for Chinese equities given the longstanding correlation 
between PPI and industrial profits given that PPI rose by 4.1% YoY in June, the highest level 
since July 2022, and was up 3.8% YoY in August. While industrial profits increased by an 
estimated 20.6% YoY in the three months to May, the highest growth since July 2021, and were 
up 15.7% YoY in first eight months of 2026.  
Still a closer look at the data shows that the reported year-to-date growth in industrial profits is 
very narrowly based, with coal, non-ferrous metals, chemicals and AI-driven semiconductors 
accounting for nearly all the gains. True, the aggregate figures look more than satisfactory. 
Sales were up 6.6% YoY in January-August while profits increased by 15.7% YoY over the same 
period. However, only 21 of the 41 sub-industries posted a profit gain in the first eight months 
of 2026. The biggest winner has been the electronics industry, which saw profits rising by 110% 
YoY in January-August, accounting for 62% of the overall profit growth. This trend is also 
captured in booming exports of chips where the data has been boosted by soaring prices. 
Exports of electronic integrated circuits have surged by 104% YoY in value terms in the first

Asia Maxima 
China: A lack of reflation 
eight months of this year but were up only 4% YoY in volume terms. This reflects the reality that 
the main driver of profits is rising prices not increasing production capacity. Still if any industry 
is going to expand capacity in China it is the electronics sector. Indeed its fixed asset 
investment increased by 8.1% YoY in January-August. But this should be seen in the context 
that total manufacturing investment declined by 2.3% YoY over the same period.  
The narrow concentration of Chinese profits is an issue for the stock market. Otherwise, the 
main feature of the latest Chinese data was confirmation that the export machine is still in full 
expansion mode. Exports were an annualised US$4.24tn based on the 12 months to August 
data, while the annualised trade surplus for the 12 months to August was a near record 
US$1.2tn. As for net exports as a driver of GDP growth, the breakdown of the real GDP growth 
shows that net exports contributed 0.9ppt or 20.8% of the real growth of 4.3% YoY in 2Q26. 
Meanwhile, returning to the issue of subdued domestic demand, the base case has been that 
household consumption has stabilised at a lower level; and that China will avoid a self-feeding 
negative equity cycle in the residential property market where, as previously discussed in this 
section, there is evidence that property prices in tier-one and tier-two cities have bottomed out. 
This is in part because loan-to-value ratios even at the peak of the property boom were a 
conservative 50%. Still if mortgage debt does not seem a systemic risk, there is more of an 
issue with plain vanilla retail loans, often distributed on loan platforms operated by China’s e-
commerce giants where younger borrowers are particularly vulnerable. Interestingly NPLs on 
household and consumer related loans are rising even as corporate NPLs are declining. Thus, 
retail NPLs on personal loans have risen from 0.6% in 2H21 to 1.4% in 2H25, while corporate 
NPLs have declined from 2.2% in 2H20 to 1.3% in 2H25, based on the ten China banks under 
Jefferies coverage. Within the personal loans category, mortgage NPLs were 0.5% in 2H25, 
while consumption loans and credit card loans NPLs were 0.6% and 1.6% respectively.  
This deterioration in retail lending has occurred in a macro context where nominal disposable 
income per capita growth has declined from 8.9% YoY in 2019 prior to the pandemic to 5.2% 
YoY in 1H26, reflecting the deflationary backdrop. The other issue is the poor employment 
market, particularly for younger people. The official overall urban unemployment rate was 5.3% 
as of August, while the unemployment rate for people aged 16-24 (excluding students) was 
18.9% in August.  
All this is evidence of the problem weak nominal growth poses for those in debt. Still, while 
President Xi Jinping has committed to boosting the role of consumption in the economy, the 
evidence remains that he is much more interested in pursuing technological upgrading and self-
reliance rather than boosting domestic demand by tax funded handouts and the like.  
Finally, from a valuation standpoint, MSCI China trades at 11.1x 2026 consensus forecast 
earnings assuming 12% earnings growth, while the CSI 300 is on 12.4x 2026 consensus 
forecast earnings assuming 22% earnings growth.

Asia Maxima 
Hong Kong: A pause to refresh 
Hong Kong: A pause to refresh  
The Fed’s first rate hike since July 2023 last quarter, and the expectations of more rate hikes 
to come, means there is likely to be a pause to refresh in Hong Kong’s residential property 
market after primary residential transaction volume reached a 22-year high in the first half of 
this year. There were 12,459 primary transactions and 30,808 secondary transactions in 1H26 
according to Midland Realty, up 34% and 44% YoY respectively. Still primary and secondary 
private residential transactions declined to 3,193 and 12,236 units last quarter.  
This upturn has also been complemented by a continuing robust trend in stock market listings. 
Jefferies estimates HK$448bn in IPOs this year, up from last year’s HK$287bn which was the 
highest in the world in 2025. IPOs have raised HK$387bn so far this year, according to Hong 
Kong Exchanges.  
Meanwhile, the Centa-City Leading Index of apartment prices is up 18.7% since bottoming in 
March 2025, though it is still 16.3% below its August 2021 peak. The major positive catalyst for 
Hong Kong property and indeed the economy remains the inflow of people, now running at 
around 130,000 a year, triggered by the government’s various talent schemes including the Top 
Talent Scheme, of which 95% are mainlanders.  
If the above is positive, a negative for sentiment was mainland press reports last quarter of the 
Chinese government imposing a 20% tax on rental income and capital gains on mainland 
residents owning overseas property, especially those assets transferred into offshore trusts 
(see Xinhua article: “China clarifies individual income tax rules for offshore trusts”, 24 July 2026) 
This follows an unveiling of new rules on outbound investment announced by China’s State 
Council on 1 June which became effective on 1 July. These were broadened for the first time 
to include individual investors. Mainland tax authorities have also reportedly started levying 
personal income tax on the returns of offshore insurance policies (see Caixin Global article: 
“China widens tax net to offshore insurance”, 5 August 2026). 
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
Still, residential property has continued to be driven by mainland buyers with an estimated up 
to 50% of buyers in the primary market mainlander in value terms and over 20% in the secondary 
market. Most of the mainlanders are buying for the long-term. Meanwhile, in a sign of improving 
confidence, the Hong Kong government has put up more land for sale as developers’ inventory 
of unsold units has continued to come down. Private residential units available for disposal, 
including units completed or under construction but not yet sold, have declined from a peak of 
98,000 units in 3Q24 to 80,000 in 2Q26.

66

 
      
Asia Maxima 
Hong Kong: A pause to refresh 
As a result, the government has sold 12 plots of land so far this calendar year with a total 
premium of HK$8.1bn, compared with three plots worth HK$3.6bn in the previous nine months 
following sales of three plots worth HK$4.9bn in 1Q25. The latest land sales include the first 
“large-scale land disposal” project in the Northern Metropolis which was sold for HK$1.03bn in 
late August to a six-company consortium. The project includes three residential sites and an 
Enterprise & Technology Park (E&TP) site, spanning a total area of about 3.6 hectares. The 
Northern Metropolis is a major 30,000-hectare development area in northern New Territories 
bordering Shenzhen.    
This renewed activity should be seen in the context of the dramatic slump in land revenues in 
recent years. Total government revenues from land sales and other land premiums have 
collapsed from a peak of HK$164.8bn or 26.6% of total revenues in FY17 ended 31 March 2018 
to HK$18.6bn or 2.7% of total revenues in FY25 ended 31 March 2026. The government’s land 
premium target for this fiscal year is HK$18bn or 2.4% of total revenues.  
If the obvious near-term risk to Hong Kong residential property remains renewed Fed tightening, 
there is no doubt of the improving fundamentals. The Hong Kong Monetary Authority’s quarterly 
survey released at the end of July shows that the number of residential mortgage loans in 
negative equity declined by 61.9% QoQ and 88.5% YoY to 4,356 cases or 0.71% of total 
borrowers at the end of 2Q26, down from 40,741 or 6.88% at the end of 1Q25.  
Meanwhile, residential rents are up more than prices, reflecting the continuing impact of the 
inflow of people. The Centa-City Rental Index has risen by 28.2% since bottoming in January 
2023. This inflow continues to fulfill two agendas from Beijing’s standpoint. It supports the 
Hong Kong economy while also diluting Cantonese influence given that most of the successful 
applicants to the talent schemes are from the mainland. 
If an upturn is under way, the office and retail segments of the property market are still under 
pressure. Still the increase in supply in Hong Kong office property peaked last year. Colliers 
projects new Grade A office completions of 1.24m sq ft in 2026 and 1.58m sq ft in 2027, down 
from 3.35m sq ft in 2025. When the turn in the office cycle comes in Hong Kong, history shows 
that it can be quite dramatic. With office rents in prime buildings already down more than 40% 
from the peak reached in early 2019, it is interesting that Grade A office rents in Central have 
risen by about 10% in the first eight months of this year, according to Jones Lang Lasalle (JLL).  
Meanwhile, the key point is that the residential market has bottomed amid rising rents and, until 
recently, declining borrowing costs. The effective mortgage rate is currently 3.25%. There is 
also continuing evidence from the official data that the downturn in domestic demand has 
stabilised. Retail sales rose by 5.8% YoY in value terms and 3.2% YoY in volume terms in the 
three months to July. Still the trend of Hong Kong people consuming in cheaper neighbouring 
Shenzhen continues. Hong Kong residents departing through land borders during weekends, 
mostly to Shenzhen, rose from an average of 179,000/day in 2023 to 299,000/day in 2025 and 
317,000/day year-to-date.  
Meanwhile, total bank deposits have risen by 40% over the past five years since 2021 to 
HK20.8tn at the end of August, with US dollar deposits surging by 75% to HK$9.4tn. By contrast, 
total loans have declined by 1.3% over the past five years. As a result, the overall loan-to-deposit 
ratio (LDR) has declined from 74.1% in June 2021 to 52.0% in August 2026, with the Hong Kong 
dollar LDR falling from 90.8% in October 2022 to 69.3% in August.  
Finally, from a valuation standpoint, MSCI Hong Kong trades on 13.7x 12-month forward 
earnings with a forecast 2026 dividend yield of 3.9%. While Hong Kong property stocks have a 
forecast 2026 dividend yield of 5.1%.

Asia Maxima 
India: Cyclical momentum 
India: Cyclical momentum  
The structural growth story remains intact with the economic data remarkably resilient given 
the obvious challenges posed by geopolitics to a country which remains a major consumer of 
energy. 
Indeed the performance has been much better than anyone was expecting at the start of this 
year. This is best captured by bank credit which grew by 18.1% YoY in mid-September. Credit 
growth is strong across all segments. But corporate lending and loans to SMEs have been 
particularly robust. Corporate loans rose by 21.6% YoY in August, while loans to micro, small 
and medium industrial enterprises were up 25.5% YoY. This trend is also supported by healthy 
deposit growth which rose by 17.3% YoY in mid-September.  
While there is a base effect partly at work the pickup in lending to SMEs suggest that the GST 
reforms and labour reforms at the end of the last year, combined with the focus of the third 
Modi government on improving the ease of doing business and removing the last vestiges of 
the “Licence Raj”, may be starting to see some dividends. These reforms were discussed in a 
previous edition of this quarterly publication (see Asia Maxima – Iran and the AI inflection point, 
6 April 2026).  
The pickup in corporate lending also suggests that the long anticipated private sector capex 
cycle may finally be happening. Another sign is the increase in the import of capital goods. The 
annualised nominal gross fixed capital formation to GDP ratio has increased from a recent low 
of 31.4% in the four quarters to December to 32.4% in the four quarters to June. While imports 
of machinery goods have risen from US$29bn in FY21 to US$62bn in FY26 and US$66bn in the 
12 months to August.  
As a result, India looks on track to achieve 6.5-7% real GDP growth and nominal GDP growth of 
around 11-12% this fiscal year ending 31 March 2027. The view of Jefferies’ India office is that 
the nominal GDP acceleration should lead to a pickup in earnings growth from 14% this fiscal 
year to 17% next fiscal year.  
The resilience of domestic demand can also be seen in GST receipts which grew by 14.8% YoY 
in August. Other evidence is the surge in power demand growth and the renewed pickup in 
residential real estate sold. Power demand growth rose from 1.8% YoY in January-March to 
9.4% YoY in April-August, while residential real estate area sold in the Top-7 cities increased by 
7% YoY in the first eight months of this calendar year, compared with a 1% YoY decline in 2025.  
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
Meanwhile, with the government having raised a better than expected US$144bn of foreign 
currency deposits from non-resident Indians (NRIs) in the FCNR-B scheme, the rupee may finally 
have bottomed having depreciated by 10.7% against the US dollar since the start of 2025. The 
RBI will likely raise rates by 25bp at its 5-7 October policy meeting with the policy repo rate

Asia Maxima 
India: Cyclical momentum 
currently at 5.25%. CPI inflation was 4.8% YoY in August. The money markets are expecting a 
25bp rate hike in both the October and December meetings to 5.75% at the end of 2026.  
Such monetary tightening makes sense given the decline in the real interest rate, and given the 
situation in the Persian Gulf. That said, the Indian government has so far done a good job 
avoiding the threat of supply disruptions. India continues to be a major buyer of discounted 
Russian oil but it has also been buying more expensive energy from the US. Russia’s share of 
India’s crude oil imports has risen from 20% in February to 56% in July and 45% in August. 
Meanwhile the fiscal deficit is projected at 4.3% of GDP this fiscal year, which is the deficit 
forecast in the February budget despite an increase in energy subsidies to protect the consumer 
in part from the rise in fuel prices. 
A further boost to consumer confidence, and people’s ability to spend, has been a booming 
organised gold lending market. This was the subject of a recent report by Jefferies’ India office 
(see India's Hidden Stimulus: The Gold Wealth Effect, 27 August 2026). The value of gold held by 
Indian households is estimated at US$3.9tn at the end of March or 24.2% of total household 
assets, while outstanding organised gold loans were US$197bn at the end of March. There is, 
therefore, huge potential for greater monetisation even though gold loans have risen by an 
annualised 32% over the past three years.  
From a stock market standpoint, the mid and small cap segment has been the most interesting 
part of the equity market. Smaller stocks have continued to outperform the big caps this year 
in spite of higher valuations. The Nifty MidCap 100 Index is trading on 22.9x 12-month forward 
earnings, compared with 17.0x for the Nifty. The Nifty Midcap 100 Index has declined by 1.9% 
year-to-date and is up 88% since the start of 2023, while the Nifty is down 13.4% year-to-date 
and up 25% since the beginning of 2023. As a result, the top 20 stocks account for a declining 
percentage of India’s total stock market capitalisation, falling from 44% to 27% since 2020. This 
is the complete opposite of the trend globally given the surge in passive investing.  
The dynamism of the small mid cap sector is extremely healthy, as are the continuing robust 
inflows into the domestic mutual fund industry. Monthly net inflows into domestic equity 
mutual funds have averaged Rs388bn (US$4.1bn) so far this year. But that inflow continues to 
be absorbed by equity issuance which has picked up again in recent months. Monthly equity 
supply declined from a peak of US$10.4bn in June 2025 to US$1.0bn in April 2026 following the 
outbreak of the Iran war but subsequently rebounded to US$9.5bn in August. This supply 
dynamic is having the practical impact of limiting the upside of the benchmark Nifty Index.  
Meanwhile, a report published by Jefferies’ India office in September titled India's New Industrial 
Revolution features six growth areas combining long-term government support and growing 
private sector participation. They are space, semiconductors, solar manufacturing, data 
centers, electronics and aerospace (see Jefferies research India’s New Industrial Revolution, 9 
September 2026). 
Government policy support is visible in these emerging sectors through the likes of incentive 
schemes, tax holidays and localisation requirements. The decision to push advanced 
manufacturing should be seen in the context of the undoubted threat to the Indian IT services 
sector posed by AI. However, employment at multinationals’ global capability centres (GCCs) 
still appears to be growing as reflected in rising demand for office space. GCC revenue has 
risen from US$64.6bn in FY24 to US$98.4bn in FY26.

Asia Maxima 
Indonesia: Surprise departures 
Indonesia: Surprise departures 
There were two notable and surprising developments last quarter in terms of economic policy. 
The first was the sudden resignation in late July of Bank Indonesia governor Perry Warjiyo who 
had been in his position since May 2018. The second was the dismissal in September of 
Finance Minister Purbaya Yudhi Sadewa. 
Perry, who had presided over an intra-meeting 25bp hike in the policy rate to 5.5% on 9 June 
and another hike to 5.75% in a regular meeting on 17-18 June, appears to have been blamed for 
sucking up excess bank liquidity by selling Bank Indonesia Rupiah Securities (SRBIs). This was 
viewed as controversial is because state-owned banks were saying that their purchases of 
SRBIs were the reason why they were not able to meet the funding requirements for President 
Prabowo Subianto’s landmark Village Cooperatives programme. The government aims to 
establish 80,000 cooperatives across rural regions.  
The Ministry of Cooperatives data shows that a total of 26,970 village cooperatives have 
already completed physical development as at the end of last quarter, with another 8,641 
cooperatives still under construction. As for the pressure on state-owned banks to fund such 
cooperatives, if state banks lend Rp3bn to each coop with a six-year tenor, the maximum loan 
amount each coop can apply for, that would amount to what a conservative banker might regard 
as Rp240tn of potential NPLs based on the targeted 80,000 coops, or 5.5% of state banks’ 
outstanding loans of Rp4,385tn as at the end of July.  
The above risk explains in significant part the de-rating of the major quoted state-owned banks 
since the start of last year. Bank Mandiri, Bank Rakyat Indonesia and Bank Negara Indonesia 
trade at 6.3x, 7.6x and 5.6x 12-month forward earnings, down from 8.7x, 9.3x and 6.5x at the 
start of 2025. Still it is worth noting that, technically, the state-owned banks are not allowed to 
lend to the village coops directly but rather to a government-owned holding company for all 
these village coops known as PT Agrinas Pangan Nusantara. The four SOE banks have reported 
a total of Rp175tn loan outstanding to Agrinas as of 30 June, according to Mandiri Securities, 
Jefferies’ Indonesia research partner. The first installment of such loans had been repaid by the 
Ministry of Finance on 25 September amounting to Rp37.7tn, comprising Rp29.2tn of principal 
repayment and Rp8.5tn of interest payment. After this repayment, the loans outstanding to 
Agrinas should have declined to Rp145.8tn.   
As for the dismissal of Purbaya, one of the issues was his request for a dividend payment from 
the state-owned sovereign wealth fund Danantara of R120tn this fiscal year to help fund the 
state budget. Under the charter of Danantara, which was set up in February 2025, all the 889 
SOEs were moved into it and Danantara receives all the dividends. These dividends totaled 
Rp114tn last year. Previously, they would have gone to the finance ministry.  
Neither the currency nor the bond market have so far reacted badly to the above personnel 
changes because both replacements are viewed as orthodox. Perry’s “temporary” replacement 
was Senior Deputy Governor Destry Damayanti who is an orthodox economist known to the 
markets. The laws require Parliament to approve the president's nominee for Bank Indonesia 
governor within one month. On this point, President Prabowo officially nominated Destry as the 
sole candidate in August, who was then sworn in as BI governor following parliamentary 
approval in early September. While the new finance minister, Suahasil Nazara, has been deputy 
finance minister since late 2019 and worked under respected former finance minister Sri 
Mulyani Indrawati.

Asia Maxima 
Indonesia: Surprise departures 
The perceived orthodoxy of the current heads of both the central bank and finance ministry 
explain the relative calm in the markets. The rupiah has depreciated by 6.6% against the US 
dollar year to date but rallied marginally last quarter by 0.1%. While the ten-year rupiah 
government bond has been remarkably resilient in the context of the sell-off in the US bond 
market last quarter. The 10-year rupiah government bond yield declined by 1bp last quarter to 
7.14%, though it is up 107bp year-to-date. This calm allowed Bank Indonesia to keep monetary 
policy on hold at its meeting in September. As for inflation, CPI rose by 3.19% YoY in August 
while core inflation was 2.92% YoY.  
Still it is a matter of time before investors refocus on government policies which have continued 
to undermine the confidence of the private sector. The most dramatic example of late, and 
perhaps the most important given Indonesia’s role as a major commodity producer, is the so-
called “export through one door” policy announced on 20 May. This requires exporters of 
strategic natural resource commodities, such as crude palm oil, coal and ferroalloys, to sell to 
a newly created state-owned entity Danantara Sumberdaya Indonesia (DSI) which will pay them 
in rupiah. The transition period began 1 June with full implementation expected by the end of 
December 2026. DSI analysed approximately 6,500 export declarations during its first three 
months of operation, according to data from the Coordinating Ministry for Economic Affairs.  
The basic macro indicators are not yet looking ominous because of the extended period of 
fiscal conservatism under Mulyani during whose more than 13 years as finance minister 
(including 4.5 years between 2005-2010 under Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono) Indonesia 
conformed to a self-imposed 3% of GDP fiscal deficit limit. Indonesia government debt to GDP 
is still only 41.3% of GDP though tax revenues are also only 10% of GDP. If the fiscal deficit is 
still projected by the government at 2.85% of GDP this year, the direction of travel is for a sharp 
deterioration driven by accelerating government spending. One example is the rising debt 
servicing ratio, in terms of interest payments as a percentage of domestic tax revenues. This 
was running around 12% of tax revenues in the early 2010s but it rose to 27% last year and is 
projected to be 25% this year based on official estimates.  
The impact of Prabowo’s populist programmes can be seen in the second quarter GDP data 
where real government consumption grew by 16% YoY, accounting for 1.1ppt or 20% of the 
reported real GDP growth of 5.3% YoY. This means private consumption, investment and net 
exports only contributed to 4.1ppt of the growth. Meanwhile tax receipts are running below 
budget forecasts raising issues about the quality of the growth. Tax revenues were up 21.7% 
YoY to Rp1,619tn in the first eight months of 2026, accounting for only 61.5% of the 
government’s full-year estimate of Rp2,631tn.    
The stock market has de-rated significantly. The 84 companies covered by Mandiri, Jefferies’ 
Indonesia research partner, trade on 9.3x 2026 earnings based on 19.2% forecast earnings 
growth this year. Still foreign investors remained net sellers last quarter, selling a net US$417m 
of Indonesian equities after selling US$1.95bn in 1Q26 and US$2.3bn in 2Q26.  
Finally, the MSCI’s threat on 28 January to move Indonesia to frontier market status unless it 
addressed free float issues remains an issue. In a recent review in late June, MSCI said it will 
continue to assess recent transparency reforms announced by the Indonesian authorities and 
will defer a decision on downgrading to its index review due in November. Indonesia now 
accounts for only 0.43% of the MSCI Emerging Market Index.

Asia Maxima 
Korea: The “super cycle” 
Korea: The “super cycle”  
The dependence on AI capex is even greater in the case of the Korean economy than in the US. 
In aggregate, manufacturing of computers, electronics and optical products contributed 1.9 
percentage points or 51% to Korea’s real GDP growth of 3.7% YoY in 2Q26. Real GDP rose by 
3.7% YoY in 2Q26, with real exports rising by 8.9% YoY driven by the exports of semiconductors, 
machinery and equipment. Still, real private consumption remained relatively weak, rising by 
2.4% YoY in 2Q26. Meanwhile, nominal GDP rose by 9.2% QoQ and 26.4% YoY in 2Q26, the 
highest YoY growth in nearly 47 years since 3Q79. This surge in the denominator caused the 
government debt to GDP ratio to decline from 51% to 48%. 
The latest Korean trade data shows the largest growth gap between exports and imports of 
goods since 1998, with exports rising by 67.5% YoY in US dollar terms in the three months to 
August or 41.2 percentage points higher than import growth of 26.3% YoY. Exports of 
semiconductors soared by 193% YoY to US$133bn in the three months to August, accounting 
for a record 44% of total exports.  
Such data explain why there are growing references to a semiconductor “super cycle”. 
Meanwhile, in terms of the geopolitical sensitivities, America and China remain Korea’s two 
largest export markets. Korean exports to China rose by 102.7% YoY to US$65.8bn in the three 
months to August, while exports to the US increased by 78.5% YoY to US$54bn over the same 
period. Exports to the US accounted for 17.8% of Korea’s annualised exports in the 12 months 
to August, up from 11.6% in the 12 months to September 2018, while China’s share of 
annualised Korean exports has declined from 27.3% to 20.4% over the same period. 
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
If exports are booming driven by the AI-triggered demand for chips, the relatively weak import 
data reflects a relatively depressed domestic economy. The booming semiconductor industry 
is capital intensive, not labour intensive, while Korean manufacturers have moved production 
to America in recent years to take advantage of incentives first implemented under the Biden 
administration’s Inflation Reduction Act in 2022. Corporate Korea has announced planned 
investments in America totaling an estimated US$228bn since late 2021. While official FDI data 
shows that Korea’s outward FDI to the US has totaled US$135bn in the past five years and 
US$19bn in 1H26, up from US$71bn in the previous five years.  
There remains a continuing lack of evidence of a domestic capex cycle. Korea’s annualised 
gross fixed capital formation as a percentage of nominal GDP has declined from 32.2% in 2Q23 
to 26.4% in 2Q26, the lowest level since 1Q77. 
The result has been political pressure on the two big semiconductor companies to announce 
increases in production capacity in Korea. The Korean government said in late June that

Asia Maxima 
Korea: The “super cycle” 
Samsung and Hynix plan to each build two new massive chip fabrication sites in South Korea’s 
southwest region valued at a combined W800tn (US$597bn). In terms of actual corporate 
announcements, SK Hynix announced in early August that its board approved W54.3tn 
(US$38bn) in investments to expand production capacity, comprising W35.2tn for new chip 
fabrication facilities in Yongin, south of Seoul, and W19.1tn for a new fab in Cheongju, central 
South Korea. 
Meanwhile, Hynix also announced in August that it would buy back and cancel W40tn 
(US$29bn) of treasury shares over a three month period starting in late August. This marks the 
largest treasury share cancellation ever conducted by a Korean listed company. Hynix also 
raised US$26.5bn in a US equity offering in July. The ADR ended last quarter at a 41% premium 
over the Korean quoted shareprice. Samsung Electronics also announced in August a 
“shareholder return plan” for 2026 worth an estimated W90-110tn, the largest ever by a Korean 
company and five times its previous high of W20.3tn set in 2020. The plan is to distribute W30tn 
in cash dividends in 3Q26 and buy back W15tn of shares.  
Aside from semiconductors, the left-wing Minjoo Government is playing its traditional 
redistributional role of seeking to suppress speculation in high-end residential property. Multi-
home owners of properties in designated speculative zones have since May faced capital gains 
tax rates up to 75%, or 82.5% including local taxes. Unsurprisingly, apartment sales in Seoul 
have declined from a recent high of 8,946 units in May to 4,589 units in August. President Lee 
Jaemyung has also banned government officials with multiple homes from participating in real 
estate policymaking. 
Such policies do not seem to be popular. If Lee is associated with the 5,000 Kospi target set in 
his successful 2025 presidential election campaign, his popularity also peaked with the stock 
market. Indeed Lee’s approval rating was only 37% in the week ended 17 September, based on 
the latest weekly poll from Gallup Korea, down sharply from a peak of 67% in April. 
Meanwhile, stock market margin lending has risen by 111% from W15.8tn at the end of 2024 to 
W33.4tn at the end of last quarter though down from a peak of W38.6tn in late June. Still, the 
real unwind following July’s sharp sell-off in semiconductor stocks has been in leveraged ETFs 
which saw huge inflows and then collapsed. Korea’s leveraged equity ETFs’ assets under 
management rose from US$8.3bn at the end of 2025 to a peak of US$50.4bn in late June and 
have since declined by US$27.6bn to US$22.8bn.  
The Bank of Korea raised the policy base rate by 50bp to 3.0% last quarter with consecutive 
rate hikes in July and August. This is in the context of forecast real GDP growth this year of 
3.3% and CPI inflation at 3.1% YoY in August.  The ten-year government bond yield is now 4.40%, 
up from 2.56% in late April 2025. 
As for the won, it remains cheap based on the real effective exchange rate. Still it has 
appreciated by 14.9% against the US dollar since bottoming this year at 1,559 on 1 July. The 
best explanation for how cheap the won became in 2Q26, in the context of Korea’s annualised 
current account surplus running at 13.1% of GDP, is capital outflows in terms of net foreign 
selling of equities in the second quarter. Thus, foreigners sold a record net W87.5tn worth of 
Korean equities in 2Q26 following net selling of W54.8tn in 1Q26. They sold a further W44.8tn 
last quarter.

Asia Maxima 
Malaysia: Still investment driven 
Malaysia: Still investment driven   
Malaysia has remained more protected from the Iran conflict as a net exporter of energy, while 
petrol and diesel are heavily subsidized. These fuel subsidies peaked at RM7.5bn per month in 
April and have been running at RM3.5-5bn per month, though they were raised again at the end 
of August. The Ministry of Finance now estimates that the fuel subsidy bill could reach RM40bn 
this year, compared with an initial budget allocation of RM15bn for 2026. 
Meanwhile the sell-off in the local government bond market has remained relatively contained 
with the ten-year yield up 54bp year to date to 4.05%. As for the ringgit it has declined by only 
0.5% year to date against the US dollar following its 10.1% appreciation last year.  
Economic momentum has been maintained with real GDP rising by 6.0% YoY in 2Q26 after the 
5.2% YoY growth recorded in calendar 2025. CIMB, Jefferies’ Malaysian research partner, 
forecasts real GDP growth of 5.0% this year. The upturn in the investment cycle has been driven 
by the AI-related thematic, namely the construction of data centers primarily in southern Johor 
close to Singapore to take advantage of cheap land and cheap energy. There have been 61 data 
center projects announced since the start of 2023 with maximum total demand of 8.35GW once 
fully developed, according to Tenaga Nasional. This is the context of total installed electricity 
generation capacity of 42GW at the end of 2025. 
Real gross fixed capital formation grew by 4.6% YoY in 2Q26, though down from 7.3% YoY in 
1Q26. As a result, annualised gross fixed capital formation has risen from 18.2% of nominal 
GDP in 4Q22 to 21.1% in 2Q26, though down from 21.4% in 1Q26. Still capital goods imports 
rose by 39.6% YoY in the three months to August, suggesting continuing investment 
momentum.  
Aside from data centers, there is another Johor-related thematic linked to the arbitrage between 
expensive Singapore and cheap Johor. That is the Rapid Transit System (RTS) linking Johor 
and Singapore which is now expected to commence in February 2027. The transport linkage is 
a key requirement for the development of the Johor-Singapore Special Economic Zone (JS-
SEZ), which it is estimated will increase Singapore’s access to industrial land by fivefold. The 
JS -SEZ is to be located mainly in the Iskandar region in Johor, potentially covering an area over 
3,500 sq km or about four times the size of Singapore. Johor recorded RM110bn of approved 
private investments in 2025. Out of this total RM60.5bn were foreign investments, compared 
with RM29bn in 2024. In 1H26 Johor recorded another RM59.4bn of approved private 
investments, including RM46.3bn of foreign investments.  
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
Meanwhile the current multiracial government, led by Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim, remains in 
power with the next general election not due until February 2028, though it can be called earlier. 
The likelihood of an election being called this year has been reduced by the coalition 
government’s loss of three state elections in Sabah last November, Johor in July and Negeri 
Sembilan on 1 August.

82

 
      
Asia Maxima 
Malaysia: Still investment driven 
The ability of the government, which was elected in November 2022, to stay in power remains 
critical given the protracted political instability which followed the defeat of UMNO, the long-
ruling party since Independence, in the general election held in May 2018. With a two-thirds 
majority in parliament, the coalition government has the means to execute on its policies. Still, 
there have been some tensions of late. There were calls from some sections of UMNO to leave 
the coalition following the sentencing in late December of former prime minister Najib Razak 
to another 15 years in prison. The judge ordered this 15-year prison term to run consecutively 
after he completes his current sentence in 2028. There was further controversy when the 
Federal Territories Pardons Board agreed on 18 September to grant Najib a conditional pardon 
allowing him to serve the rest of his current sentence under house arrest until August 2028. 
(This is the previous sentence in 2022 initially for 12 years in prison but halved to six years by 
then king in 2024.) Najib subsequently applied in late September for a stay of his new 15-year 
sentence pending further appeals.  
Anwar has to tread warily not to alienate the Malays, given the ever-present racial tensions in 
Malaysian politics, as he remains in coalition with the Chinese-dominated Democratic Action 
Party, the biggest party in the coalition with 40 parliamentary seats. Anwar was confirmed in 
May last year as leader of his party, the PKR, for another three years. But with only 28 seats 
currently in parliament the party continues to rely on its chief ally, the DAP.  
UMNO, as another member of the ruling coalition, has 26 seats. Still UMNO always has the 
option to revive Muafakat Nasional, a short-lived pact that the party formed with the Islamist 
opposition party PAS in 2019. PAS is the largest party in the parliament with 43 seats. The Johor 
state election in July marked the beginning of a broader electoral cycle. A state election in 
Malacca is also due to be held on or before 22 November following the recent dissolution of 
the state assembly, while state election in Sarawak must be held on or before February 2027.  
From a fiscal standpoint the Iran triggered surge in energy subsidies has undone some of the 
benefits of recent fiscal reforms, such as the introduction of an expanded sales and service tax 
(SST) in July last year. Meanwhile, total government debt to GDP has increased from 52.4% at 
the end of 2019 to 65.4% at the end of 2Q26. The fiscal deficit is projected at 3.5% of GDP in 
2026 based on the forecast in the budget announced last October.  
Meanwhile, the positive trend in foreign direct investment continues. Approved FDI was 
US$51.4bn last year, of which US$24.8bn was in the manufacturing sector and US$26bn in the 
services sector. Approved FDI totalled US$31.1bn in 1H26 with US$8bn in the manufacturing 
sector and US$21.3bn in the services sector. Exports were up 42% YoY in US dollar terms in 
January-August. 
As for the stock market, local institutions bought a net RM19.5bn of Malaysian stocks last year.  
This net buying has continued this year though they have so far bought only a net RM5.1bn. 
This has helped absorb net foreign selling of RM22.6bn last year, and a net RM6.5bn so far this 
year. The KLCI trades on 14.0x 12-month forward earnings, compared with a ten-year average 
of 14.9x. KLCI earnings are forecast by CIMB to rise by 7.6% in 2026 and 4.2% in 2027. The 
projected dividend yield is a respectable 4.2%.  
As regards monetary policy, Bank Negara has remained on hold following the cut in the 
overnight policy rate (OPR) by 25bp to 2.75% in July 2025. Prior to that easing, the policy rate 
had been at 3% since May 2023. CPI inflation was 1.9% YoY in August with inflation forecast by 
CIMB to be 1.9% in 2026. Meanwhile, foreign ownership of the stock market fell from 19.2% at 
the end of February to a new all-time low of 17.8% at the end of September.

Asia Maxima 
Philippines: A perfect storm 
Philippines: A perfect storm  
The Philippines remains a prime casualty of the Iran War given the country’s vulnerability to 
higher energy prices and dependence on imported oil combined with the almost complete lack 
of fuel or electricity subsidies. Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) stated in an off-cycle meeting 
in late March that it expected inflation to average 5.1% in 2026, well above the ceiling of its 
inflation target range of 2-4%, compared with only 3.2% projected at the start of this year.  
This forecast has so far proved correct. Inflation has averaged 5.2% in the first eight months of 
this year and was 6.1% YoY in August. The central bank subsequently raised the Target Reverse 
Repurchase (RRP) rate by a further 25bp to 5.0% in August. There have been 75bp of rate hikes 
this year after a 25bp cut in February. The BSP raised the 2026 average inflation forecast to 
6.4% in June, while inflation is expected to average 4.5% next year before slowing to 3.1% in 
2028. Still the central bank lowered this year’s inflation forecast to 6.1% in August, though the 
inflation forecast for 2027 rose to 5.4%.  
The need to tighten reflects not only inflation but also the vulnerability of the peso given the 
country’s twin deficits. The peso broke the psychologically key 60 level against the US dollar in 
late April and is down 6.1% year to date. Reflecting investor concerns, the ten-year peso 
government bond yield is 7.62%, an increase of 155bp so far this year though still down from a 
recent peak of 7.81% reached in May. In terms of trade deficits, the trade deficit was an 
annualised US$58.4bn or 11.9% of GDP in August. While the current account deficit was an 
annualised 4.5% of GDP in 2Q26.  
The energy driven price crunch follows a collapse in government infrastructure spending. Real 
government consumption expenditure rose by 8.3% YoY in 2Q26, down from 18.7% YoY in 1Q25 
though up from 0.7% YoY in 4Q25. While real gross fixed capital formation collapsed by 13.7% 
YoY in 2Q26, driven by a 32.4% YoY collapse in public construction in 2Q26. By contrast, private 
construction investment rose by 2.5% YoY in 2Q26. As a result, real GDP growth was only 2.3% 
YoY in 2Q26, the slowest growth in five years.  
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
The precipitous decline in government spending on infrastructure is the result of a corruption-
related scandal previously discussed in this quarterly publication. But it now seems clear that 
the extremely weak investment trend has also reflected a loss in private-sector business 
confidence. The BSP’s business confidence index has turned negative since March and was a 
negative 10.9 in August, though up from -20.3 in July and a low of -35.8 in April. Meanwhile, the 
32.4% YoY collapse in government spending on infrastructure in 2Q26 reflects officials’ fear of 
corruption charges. 
The recent surge in energy costs, combined with the lack of subsidies, pose headwinds for what 
remains a consumption-driven economy. There is also the related issue that the Middle East

Asia Maxima 
Philippines: A perfect storm 
accounted for 18% of overseas workers’ cash remittances or US$6.6bn of the US$36.1bn total 
in the 12 months to July. Another 20% of remittances are from sea-based workers, be they on 
oil tankers or cargo ships, who will also have been impacted by the Iran conflict. 
All of the above is not positive for a stock market which remains as cheap as it has ever been.  
The PSEI trades on 8.4x 12-month forward earnings, compared with a historical average of 
15.1x since 2003, according to Jefferies’ Philippine research partner, Regis. This valuation 
includes port operator International Container Terminal (ICTSI), which now accounts for 27% of 
the local index and trades on 20.8 times 12-month forward earnings. If ICTSI is removed the 
valuation of the market is 6.9x.  
The 36 companies covered by Regis are forecast to report 7.0% earnings growth in 2026 with a 
projected dividend yield of 5.0%. The Regis universe also has a forecast RoE this year of 16.5%. 
The forecast earnings growth has been downgraded from 9.5% since the start of this year.  
Meanwhile, foreign ownership of all Philippine equities was 21.3% in August, though up from a 
record low of 18.4% in September 2025. Average daily trading volume is only US$104m year-
to-date. Foreigners have remained net sellers year to date, after selling a net US$883m in 2025. 
They have sold a net US$536m so far in 2026.  
As regards domestic institutions, they have remained heavily invested in fixed income while 
they have also increased overseas equity exposure. The two main government pension funds 
now have 30% of their equity portfolios in overseas (mainly US) equities and have permission 
to raise that to 50%. If this is a negative from the standpoint of the domestic stock market, the 
cheap valuations mean that listed companies have been buying back more shares. Share 
buybacks rose from P19bn in 2024 to P49bn in 2025, though they have slowed to P21bn year 
to date given the economic headwinds.  
The Philippines, with its large call center segment, remains vulnerable to disruption from AI. But 
so far at least there remains a lack of evidence of any such impact. BPO revenues were 
US$40bn last year and are projected to rise to US$42.3bn in 2026. Nationwide BPO net office 
take-up was 54,000 sqm in 2Q26. One explanation for this resilience is that AI is allowing 
English speaking call centers to handle non-English language services in real time. Remittance 
growth, another major driver of purchasing power, also remains resilient. Cash remittances 
were up 2.3% YoY to US$20.4bn in the first seven months of 2026.  
Meanwhile, politically the continuing feud between the Marcos and Duterte clans is heating up 
ahead of the May 2028 presidential election. Vice President Sara Duterte was impeached by 
the House of Representatives in May with an impeachment court case now launched in the 
Senate against Duterte. But the prevailing view is that it will be hard to obtain a conviction since 
it will require the pro Marcos camp to secure a two thirds majority in the Upper House which 
seems unlikely. And such a conviction is what is required to prevent the vice president from 
running in 2028. Still the Senate impeachment court voted on 23 September to lower the 
conviction threshold from 16 votes (two-thirds of 24 senator-judges) to 14 votes (two-thirds of 
20 active senators) as four of the 24 senators have been unable to participate in the 
proceedings for various reasons.   
So the stakes are extremely high for both sides. For now Duterte is leading in the polls with 49% 
support. Meanwhile her father, former president Rodrigo Duterte and the predecessor to current 
President Ferdinand Marcos Jr., remains in the Hague awaiting trial in the International Criminal 
Court where he has been since March 2025. The trial is scheduled to officially start on 30 
November. Meanwhile, under the Constitution, Marcos cannot run for a second term in office.

Asia Maxima 
Singapore: Credit acceleration 
Singapore: Credit acceleration   
Singapore’s real GDP grew by a better than expected 5.9% YoY in 2Q26, driven primarily by the 
AI capex boom, with the export-driven manufacturing sector expanding by 12.5% YoY in 2Q26. 
The growth trajectory remains a positive surprise given that the Ministry of Trade and Industry 
(MTI) had been forecasting growth in only the 2-4% range for 2026. 
As elsewhere in the region, AI capex-related demand for tech products has played the key role. 
Non-oil domestic exports rose by 29.4% YoY in US dollar terms in the three months to August 
while electronics exports soared by 116.6% YoY in US dollar terms over the same period. The 
government forecasters’ continuing caution on the growth outlook has been explained by 
concerns about inflationary pressures stemming from the conflict in the Middle East with 
officials warning, understandably, that the full impact has not yet fed through. Still the MTI 
revised its 2026 real GDP growth forecast significantly to 4.5-5.5% in the Economic Survey of 
Singapore released on 11 August along with the full 2Q26 GDP data.  
Meanwhile, the Singapore residential property market has continued to stabilise after an 
extended cooling off period triggered by the Additional Buyer’s Stamp Duty (ABSD) rates for 
foreigners and entities or trusts purchasing residential property. This was increased in April 
2023 from 30% and 35%, respectively, to a massive 60% and 65%. The private residential 
property price index rose by 2.9% YoY in 2Q26 while the private residential property rental index 
rose by 1.7% YoY. 
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
There remains one area of buoyant activity, namely developer sales in the mass residential 
property market. New private residential units sold by developers, excluding executive 
condominiums, totaled 10,951 units in 2025, up 65% from 6,626 units in 2024. Developer sales 
were 5,047 units in the first eight months of this year. The pickup in activity has continued to 
be supported by lower rates. The three-month compounded Singapore Overnight Rate Average 
(SORA), the key benchmark that banks use to price floating-rate mortgages, is now 1.23%, down 
from a recent high of 3.76% in November 2023, though up from a recent low of 1.02% in late 
April. Meanwhile, foreign purchases of individual properties have collapsed since the doubling 
of stamp duty. Foreigners accounted for less than 1% of non-landed new private home sales in 
August, down from a peak of 22% in December 2022. 
Inflation concerns had all but disappeared prior to the onset of the Iran war with headline CPI 
and core CPI rising by 1.2% YoY and 1.4% YoY in February, though up from a low of 0.5% YoY 
and 0.3% YoY respectively in August 2025. Still a further acceleration is now under way. 
Headline CPI rose by 2.3% YoY in August and core inflation by 2.2% YoY. The Monetary Authority 
of Singapore (MAS) tightened monetary policy in April and raised its forecasts for both headline 
and core CPI inflation to average 1.5-2.5% in 2026, up from 1.0-2.0% previously. While in the 
latest monetary policy review in July, the MAS said it will increase again the rate of appreciation

Asia Maxima 
Singapore: Credit acceleration 
of the S$NEER policy band “very slightly” with the extent of the increase smaller than the 
tightening in April, while its 2026 inflation forecasts are maintained.  
As for domestic demand, total retail sales rose by 2.8% YoY in value terms in the three months 
to July and are now 6.8% above the December 2017 pre-Covid peak. However, total tourist 
arrivals are still below 2019 levels reflecting in part the negative aspect of a strong currency. 
The Singapore dollar has, for example, appreciated by 68% against the Japanese yen since 
March 2020 and by 15% against the US dollar. Tourist arrivals declined by 1.7% YoY to 11.43m 
in the first eight months of this year, which is 11% below the pre-Covid arrivals of 12.86m in 
January-August 2019.  
After an extended downturn credit growth has turned positive since September 2024 and is now 
accelerating markedly. System loans, including loans to residents and non-residents, rose by 
16.1% YoY in August, the fastest loan growth since the current data set began in July 2021, 
while deposit growth was 6.6% YoY in August. Corporate loans rose by 16.0% YoY, also the 
strongest growth rate since the data began in July 2021. The loan-to-deposit ratio bottomed at 
65.7% last August, the lowest level since at least 2010, and has since risen to 71.6% in August. 
The main attraction of the Singapore stock market has remained as a dividend play backed by 
a strong currency, though the acceleration in credit growth has undoubtedly improved the 
growth story for the banking sector which accounts for 61.6% of the MSCI Singapore Index. The 
Singapore market trades on 17.3x 12-month forward earnings, based on consensus forecast 
2026 and 2027 earnings growths of 10.3% and 11.8%, with a forecast 2026 RoE of 12%. The 
projected dividend yield for 2026 is 3.5% while the Reit sector has a forecast average dividend 
yield of 6.2% based on the ten Reits covered by Jefferies’ Singapore office.  
Meanwhile, the authorities launched last year a programme to raise the profile of the local stock 
market commensurate with Singapore’s status as a global financial sector. One aspect of this 
is an initiative to revive capital market activity. There were 15 IPO listings in Singapore last year 
and another ten year-to-date, raising S$2.95bn and S$1.41bn respectively. This initiative is in 
the context of average daily trading volume having declined from S$2.36bn in 2007 to S$1.03bn 
in 2023; though in a sign of progress it is running at S$2.07bn year-to-date. 
From an exchange rate standpoint, the Singapore dollar has appreciated by 6.4% against the 
US dollar since the Fed commenced its credit tightening cycle in March 2022, and is up 0.6% 
against the US dollar year-to-date. The currency has also outperformed the Asia Dollar Index by 
14.3% since March 2022. The currency’s fundamental strength is in the context of a current 
account surplus running at an annualised 17.7% of GDP in 2Q26. Foreign direct investment also 
remains robust. Net FDI was an annualized S$67bn or 8.1% of GDP in the four quarters to 2Q26. 
Meanwhile, the Singapore dollar 10-year government bond yield of 2.45% still looks attractive 
for conservative money focused primarily on wealth preservation in a strong currency. The 10-
year Singapore government bond yield is now at a record 283bp below the US 10-year Treasury 
yield.

Asia Maxima 
Taiwan: Boom times further extended 
Taiwan: Boom times further extended  
The accelerating AI capex arms race has continued to contribute to boom-like conditions in the 
Taiwan economy. Export orders grew by 64.2% YoY to US$296bn in the three months to August 
while total orders for the first eight months of the year were US$705bn, up 55% YoY. This 
marked the highest annual growth on a three-month average basis since the data series began 
in 1984.  
This data is testimony to the massive investments being made on the AI-related data center 
ramp-up, as also reflected in TSMC’s estimate that it will increase its capex this year to a 
massive US$60-64bn, up from US$41bn last year. Sherman Shang, tech analyst at Fubon 
research, Jefferies’ Taiwan research partner, expects that TSMC’s capex will increase to US$80-
85bn in 2027. Sherman has TSMC on 22.8x 2026 earnings based on 64.2% forecast earnings 
growth in 2026. He estimates that AI will account for 31% of TSMC’s revenues this year.  
TSMC revenues rose by 39.3% YoY to NT$3.39tn in the first eight months of this year. The AI-
driven ongoing transition from “training” to so-called “inference” means continuing focus on 
ASIC chips. The other related issue, as the focus in AI shifts from large language models to 
agents, is that computational bottlenecks are shifting from GPU to CPU. Fubon has estimated 
the CPU/GPU ratio to converge gradually to 1:1, whereas previously the ratio was 1:4 or 1:8, 
while in certain applications the number of CPUs will exceed GPUs.  
Meanwhile, Taiwan’s real GDP rose by 12.9% YoY in 2Q26, the second fastest quarterly growth 
rate in nearly 48 years after the 15.4% YoY growth recorded in 1Q26. Exports of integrated 
circuits totaled an annualised US$270bn or 26.9% of nominal GDP in the 12 months to August. 
The government in August increased the official real GDP forecast for this year from 9.64% to 
11.05%, the highest growth rate in 39 years.  
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
There has been a dramatic change in the composition of the export data. Taiwan exports to the 
US are now greater than China’s exports to the US for the first time in 35 years, based on the 
US Census Bureau’s data. US imports of goods from China declined by 19% YoY to US$156.4bn 
in the first seven months of 2026, while US imports from Taiwan surged by 60% YoY to 
US$162.3bn over the same period. By contrast Taiwan exports to China/HK accounted for 
24.6% of total exports in the 12 months to June, the lowest level in more than 25 years, and 
were 24.7% in the 12 months to August, a decline that in part reflects continuing US restrictions 
on selling advance semiconductors to China and in part China’s growing ability to source chips 
domestically. 
The US has also become the top destination for Taiwan outbound investment in the first eight 
months of this year. Taiwan’s approved outward investment to the US totaled US$23.7bn in the 
first eight months of 2026, accounting for 38% of the total outward investment of US$63.1bn.

Asia Maxima 
Taiwan: Boom times further extended 
By contrast, only US$660m or 1% of the total went to China. There has also been a continuing 
pickup in domestic investment, though the rate of growth has slowed. Real gross fixed capital 
formation rose by 11.4% YoY in 2Q26, down from 18.4% YoY in 1Q25 though up from 4.3% YoY 
in 4Q25. While annualised gross fixed capital formation as a percentage of nominal GDP was 
25.2% in 2Q26, up from 21.2% in 2Q18 though down from a peak of 28.4% in 1Q23. 
Meanwhile credit growth has also turned up. System loans rose by 10.5% YoY in August, up 
from 5.6% YoY in November 2025, with claims on the private sector rising by 10.6% YoY. The 
private sector includes households, private enterprises, social security insurance, pension 
funds and non-profit institutions. Still mortgage loan growth slowed to 4.6% YoY in August. This 
reflects seven rounds of property cooling measures introduced by the central bank since 2024.  
Under those measures, the maximum loan-to-value (LTV) ratio for a second-home purchase 
has been lowered to 50% while financing for third homes and purchases made under corporate 
names is capped at 30% of a property’s value. As a result, the share of real estate loans in 
banks’ overall lending portfolios fell to 34.2% in August, the lowest level in 20 years. Still the 
central bank has made some adjustments this year, easing the LTV cap for second-home 
purchases to 60% in March and then to 70% in mid-September.  
As for the exchange rate, the NT dollar has declined by 1.3% against the US dollar year to date 
while the central bank remains on hold with the policy discount rate at 2.0%. The currency 
remains extremely cheap relative to the current account surplus. The real effective exchange 
rate (REER) has declined by 27.7% since the start of 1994 despite an average current account 
surplus over the past ten years of 14.1% of GDP and a record annualised 23.7% in 2Q26.  
Politically, DPP President Lai Ching-te’s approval rating declined to a record low of 31% in 
August 2025, though it has since rebounded to 46.4% in August according to My Formosa polls. 
Lai faces the challenge posed by a relatively new KMT chairwoman Cheng Li-wun. She has 
opposed Lai’s proposal to increase defence spending to above 3% of GDP in 2026 and to 5% by 
2030. Meanwhile Washington has put on hold, since the Xi-Trump summit in Beijing in May, a 
plan to sell Taiwan weapons worth up to U$14bn.  
The attack on Iran since the end of February has triggered a revival of questioning, particularly 
in the US, about whether China would take advantage of America’s pre-occupation with the 
Middle East to launch a long-feared invasion of Taiwan. Such an outlook remains far-fetched.  
China’s preferred approach is to “absorb” Taiwan through peaceful engagement. For this 
reason, it makes no sense for Beijing to escalate tensions if it believes the KMT has a decent 
chance of winning the next presidential election due to be held in early 2028. This is certainly 
the case. The KMT already enjoys, in partnership with the Taiwan People’s Party (TPP), a 62-
seat majority in the 113-seat Legislative Yuan, which it took advantage of to block the DPP 
President Lai Ching-te’s efforts to increase defence spending. The DPP has been in power since 
2016 but throughout this period Beijing has refused to have any direct contact with it.

Asia Maxima 
Thailand: Nominal glimmers 
Thailand: Nominal glimmers   
The Thai market has continued to benefit from the perception of political stability after 
Bhumjaithai led by Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul won a surprise landslide victory in the 
general election in February, as support for Thaksin’s Pheu Thai collapsed marking the likely 
end of the Thaksin political era. Bhumjaithai won 192 of the 500 seats in the House of 
Representatives, compared with only 74 for Pheu Thai. Bhumjaithai subsequently formed a 291-
seat coalition government with Pheu Thai and some other smaller parties. 
The Thai stock market viewed the result as positive since it also removed the risk of a victory 
by the People’s Party, the opposition party which won the most votes in the last general election 
held in 2023 when it was named the Move Forward Party and was subsequently banned and 
renamed. 
As a result, the risk of a political confrontation between the opposition and the pro monarchy 
establishment has been removed for now with the Bhumjaithai-led coalition looking positioned 
to enjoy a full four-year term. Anutin has also appointed a government with competent 
technocrats.   
Still the government has gone for a domestic demand push with renewed handouts. The cabinet 
in May approved a package of cash handout programmes under the name “Thais Help Thais 
Plus”. This included two sub-programmes: the Khon La Khrueng Plus copayment scheme and 
additional cash support on welfare cards. The budget for both programmes was Bt120bn and 
Bt53bn respectively. For the co-payment scheme, the government provided Bt4,000 
(Bt1,000/month for four months) to 30m people. Participants paid only 40% of the price of 
eligible purchases with the government subsidising the remaining 60%. Spending was allowed 
from 1 June to 30 September, with daily and monthly subsidy limits of Bt200 and Bt1,000 per 
person. Still the Cabinet approved on 22 September a two-month extension of the co-payment 
scheme through November while raising the welfare card support for October. The extension 
will use the Bt43bn remaining from the first phase of the programme.   
Index movement 
 
Source: Bloomberg 
 
The focus on handouts is in the context of an economy which continues to drift. The structural 
backdrop to weak consumption, and the related neo-deflationary environment of recent years, 
remains the overhang of household debt. It has been hard for debt levels to decline given the 
lack of nominal growth though that may now be changing. 
Thus, nominal GDP growth was below real GDP growth for four consecutive quarters in 2025. 
Still it was marginally higher in 1Q26, rising by 2.9% YoY compared with real GDP growth of 
2.8% YoY. More interestingly, real GDP growth slowed to 1.9% YoY in 2Q26 while nominal GDP 
rose by 3.9% YoY. This was the strongest nominal growth since 4Q24.    
Credit growth remains anaemic though it has begun to pick up. Depository corporations’ loans 
rose by 1.6% YoY in August, up from 0.1% YoY in February. NPLs and “special mention” loans

Asia Maxima 
Thailand: Nominal glimmers 
accounted for 10.3% of all retail loans in 2Q26 of which only 3.2% were NPLs. Household debt 
was 85.2% of GDP at the end of 2Q26, of which mortgage debt comprised only an estimated 
31%. So, most of the household debt is unsecured, which has been the source of the structural 
problem.  
Tisco, Jefferies’ Thai research partner, forecasts real GDP growth of only 2.1% in 2026. The 
fiscal deficit is projected at 4.4% of GDP this year with a government debt-to-GDP ratio at 67.9% 
at the end of August. Thailand is on course at the current growth rate to hit its government debt 
to GDP ceiling of 70% in around one year. Indeed the cabinet approved in early May an 
emergency decree allowing the government to borrow up to Bt400bn. The decree was then 
passed by the Senate at the end of August. The government estimates that the decree will 
increase the public debt to GDP ratio to 68.2%. While including the impact of other borrowing 
the ratio may rise to 69.9% by the end of next fiscal year ending 30 September 2027. So the 
pressure on the government to lift the debt ceiling is rising.  
The positive for the stock market this year has been a return of foreign buyers, in part as a result 
of the perceived renewed political stability and in part because of a rally in bank stocks based 
on improved shareholder returns with dividend payout ratios rising from 43% to 66% since the 
start of 2024. As a result, banks have outperformed the SET by 41% since the start of 2024 and 
are up 55% in absolute terms. This is in spite of the lack of credit growth. The banks have a 
forecast dividend yield this year of 6%.    
As for foreign investors, they have been net sellers of equities for 12 of the past 15 quarters. 
But so far this year they have bought a net Bt25bn worth of Thai stocks after selling a net 
Bt447bn since 1Q23. The stock market trades at 15.5x 2026 forecast earnings excluding AI 
play Delta Electronics, based on a 4% forecast 2026 earnings growth, according to Tisco.  
Meanwhile, the Bank of Thailand has kept monetary policy unchanged since it cut the policy 1-
day repo rate by 25bp to 1.0% in February. Further rate cuts seem highly unlikely. Indeed the 
next move is more likely to be a rate hike. CPI rose by 2.53% YoY in August while core CPI was 
up 1.44% YoY. This means real rates, deflated by CPI, are a negative 1.53%.  
As regards the exchange rate, the baht has weakened by 6.1% against the US dollar year to 
date. This is in the context of Thailand recording an annualised current account surplus of 
US$7.7bn or 1.2% of GDP in the 12 months to August, down from an annualised surplus of 
US$31.7bn or 5.6% of GDP in the 12 months to September 2025. Meanwhile, Chinese imports 
continue to surge. Imports from China rose by 34% YoY in US dollar terms last year and were 
up 36% YoY in the first eight months of 2026, with Chinese imports accounting for 31.2% of 
total annualised imports in the 12 months to August. If Chinese imports are surging, the Anutin 
government is also seeking increased foreign investment from China in a move away from 
Thailand’s traditional dependence on FDI from Japan. Approved FDI from China rose 14% YoY 
last year to a record Bt198bn, though it declined to Bt50bn in 1H26. By contrast, approved FDI 
from Japan totaled Bt67bn in 2025 and Bt35bn in 1H26.
